[PARTIAL] Bayesian spectral line-profile inference (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-25
          Second item of the next external-roadmap-table batch (items
          21-25); item 24 (P0, live calibrated spectroscopy) landed first
          since this item consumes its spectrum-acquisition machinery.
          `line_profile.py` (Voigt/Gaussian forward model), `line_profile_
          fit.py` (optimisation + emcee posterior sampling), and
          `line_profile_eval.py` (posterior coverage + line-parameter
          residuals, split purely for the 500-line guideline) are
          genuinely greenfield -- no Voigt-profile or line-fitting code
          existed anywhere in this codebase before this session.

          The boundary with item 24's `spectroscopy_calibration.py` is
          deliberate and stated in both modules' docstrings: that module's
          `find_candidate_lines`/`independent_redshift_from_lines` detect a
          candidate line's approximate WAVELENGTH and, where possible, its
          rest-frame identity, but explicitly do not fit its physical
          SHAPE -- that is this item's job. `line_profile_fit.py` mirrors
          `microlensing_fit.py` closely (`differential_evolution` ->
          `minimize` point estimate, then `emcee` posterior sampling with
          the same 50-autocorrelation-time convergence discipline), reusing
          the exact same `_require_emcee()` lazy-import pattern -- emcee is
          already a `research`-extra dependency from item 15, no
          `pyproject.toml` change needed here. `amplitude` enters the model
          LINEARLY (`continuum + amplitude * voigt_profile(...)`), exactly
          like microlensing's `f_source`/`f_blend`, so it is solved by
          weighted linear least squares at every nonlinear step
          (`solve_linear_amplitude`) rather than searched over, turning a
          4-parameter fit into a 3-parameter one (`center`, `sigma`,
          `gamma`) -- the identical reduction microlensing_fit.py already
          established for its own two linear flux parameters.

          `line_profile_eval.py` reuses `microlensing_eval.CoverageTrial`,
          `posterior_coverage`, `sbc_ranks`, AND `parameter_bias` all
          UNCHANGED by direct import -- their shape (a truth dict, a fitted
          posterior's per-level credible intervals, optionally its raw
          samples) is generic across any parametric model, confirmed by
          this session actually using them for a completely different
          physical model with no code changes to `microlensing_eval.py`
          at all.

          `line_parameter_residuals`'s real-value comparison turned out
          BETTER than this plan anticipated: rather than needing a separate
          SDSS `galSpecLine`-style value-added-catalog query (an open
          question this plan carried from item 24), a real spec-lite FITS
          downloaded this session (`spec-0266-51630-0336.fits`, the same
          file `sdss.fetch_spectrum` already fetches) was found, live, to
          carry a genuine `SPZLINE` extension -- SDSS's own per-line
          pipeline fit (`LINENAME`, `LINEWAVE`, `LINEZ`, `LINESIGMA` in
          km/s, `LINEAREA`, `LINEEW`, plus error columns), needing no
          separate query at all. `surveys/sdss.py` gained one additive
          function, `extract_sdss_line_measurements` (`cone_search`/
          `extract_sdss_spectrum` unchanged), which also converts
          `LINESIGMA`'s km/s velocity-dispersion convention to the
          Angstrom units `line_profile.LineProfileParams` uses, and treats
          a genuinely undetected/out-of-window line (`LINEZ=LINESIGMA=
          LINEAREA=0.0` exactly, confirmed live for `Ly_alpha`/`C_IV` on a
          low-z galaxy spectrum where those UV lines fall outside the
          observed range) as NOT FIT and excludes it -- the same
          "unavailable, not a fabricated zero" discipline `extract_sdss_
          spectrum`'s `ELODIE_TEFF` handling already established.

          Two real bugs were found and fixed this session via regression
          tests: (1) `fit_line_profile` broadcast the `continuum` argument
          against `wavelength`'s shape BEFORE validating that `wavelength`/
          `flux`/`error` have equal lengths, so a genuinely mismatched
          input raised numpy's own opaque `ValueError` instead of this
          module's `LineProfileError` -- fixed by validating lengths first.
          (2) A Voigt-profile unit-area test at a Lorentzian-dominated
          `gamma=0.8` truncated its numerical integration range too
          tightly (`+/-50`), understating the true area by ~1% from the
          profile's genuinely slow `~1/x^2` tails -- a test-precision issue
          caught the same way the item-24 bugs were, by actually running
          the check rather than assuming a plausible-looking tolerance was
          adequate; fixed by widening the integration range, not by
          loosening the tolerance to hide it.

          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or `evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_line_profile.py` (16 cases: parameter
          validation, Voigt-profile symmetry/unit-area/Gaussian-limit
          checks, and `model_flux` emission/absorption/continuum-shape
          behaviour), `tests/test_line_profile_fit.py` (14 cases: linear-
          amplitude recovery, bounds derivation including the center-hint
          narrowing, point-estimate recovery of known emission and
          absorption lines, the mismatched-continuum-length regression
          case, the proposal seam, and posterior convergence/credible-
          interval-containment checks -- module-gated with `pytest.
          importorskip("emcee", ...)`, the same pattern `test_microlensing_
          fit.py` already uses), `tests/test_line_profile_eval.py` (7
          cases: coverage-study metric reporting and low-bias recovery,
          real-released-line recovery with a synthetic injection, the
          out-of-range-lines-counted-not-dropped case, and one `live`-
          marked case fitting a real downloaded SDSS spectrum against its
          own real `SPZLINE` measurements), and `tests/test_surveys_sdss.py`
          (extended with 3 new cases for `extract_sdss_line_measurements`:
          a real detected line, the undetected-line-exclusion case, and a
          missing-extension case).


[PARTIAL] GW-conditioned kilonova counterpart inference (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-25
          Third item of the next external-roadmap-table batch (items
          21-25); taken after items 24/25 by user choice as the lowest
          new-connector-risk of the remaining three (21/22/23), since its
          core model/eval validate fully against already-real, already-
          credential-free connectors (`gw.py`, `surveys/alerce.py`) without
          depending on any new, unverified one. `kilonova.py` (generalized-
          Arnett ejecta light-curve model) and `kilonova_eval.py`
          (counterpart recall at a fixed telescope budget + distance-
          conditioned calibration, split purely for the 500-line
          guideline) are genuinely greenfield -- no kilonova/Arnett-
          formalism code existed anywhere in this codebase before this
          session.

          Every equation in `kilonova.py` was verified this session against
          the RAW arXiv HTML/LaTeX source of Villar et al. 2017 (ApJL 851,
          L21, arXiv:1710.11576v2, Eqs. 1-5), fetched and read directly --
          not reproduced from memory, and not trusted from an AI-summarized
          excerpt either, after that summarized pass was caught garbling a
          coefficient once already this session (see below). A real,
          load-bearing bug was found and fixed via a direct numerical
          sanity check (not just a plausible-looking test passing): a
          first-pass fixed-grid trapezoid integration of Eq. 3's Arnett
          integral put a realistic two-component GW170817-like model
          roughly 5-6 AB magnitudes too bright (12-14 mag instead of the
          real ~17-18 mag at 40 Mpc). The root cause was NOT the grid
          resolution (a second theory, also tested and also wrong) but
          genuine, verified physics: the heating-rate bracket (Eq. 1)
          transitions sharply on the ~0.11s timescale around t0=1.3s, and
          because `L_in` decays only as a slow POWER law (not
          exponentially) afterward, the early-time (t'~10-1000s) region
          genuinely dominates the integral's total value at realistic
          ejecta masses -- confirmed directly by comparing a fixed-grid
          trapezoid against `scipy.integrate.quad` with the t0 breakpoint
          given explicitly (which resolves the sharp feature correctly and
          returns an even LARGER integral than the coarse grid, ruling out
          "coarse grid overestimates" as the explanation). The actual
          finding: this model's `L_bol` scales strongly SUPER-linearly with
          `m_ej` (roughly `L ~ m_ej^2.2` near t~1 day, a real, verified
          consequence of `t_d` itself scaling with `sqrt(m_ej)` -- heavier,
          more diffusive ejecta retain more early-injected energy by any
          fixed later epoch), and the original sanity-check masses
          (0.02/0.04 solar masses) were simply ~5-20x too large versus
          real GW170817-like fits (~0.001-0.01 solar masses per
          component) -- at `m_ej=0.001`, `L_bol(1 day)` lands at ~9e41
          erg/s, the correct real order of magnitude. A SEPARATE, smaller
          discrepancy was also found and NOT silently "corrected": the raw
          arXiv HTML renders Eq. 4 as `T = [L/(4*pi*sigma_SB^2*v_ej^2*
          t^2)]^(1/4)` with `sigma_SB` SQUARED, but Eq. 5's own second
          branch -- the identical physical relation solved for R instead
          of T -- uses a single `sigma_SB`, and a squared Stefan-Boltzmann
          constant is also dimensionally inconsistent with the
          Stefan-Boltzmann law itself; treated as an arXiv LaTeXML
          HTML-conversion artifact in Eq. 4 (documented as such, not
          silently assumed), with the dimensionally-consistent
          single-`sigma_SB` form implemented.

          `THERMALIZATION_A`/`B`/`D` (Eq. 2) are stated, fixed fiducial
          constants, NOT the full mass/velocity-dependent interpolation of
          Barnes et al. 2016's Table 1 the paper's own text describes --
          that separate paper's table was not extracted this session, an
          explicit, stated simplification matching `agn_changepoint.
          tde_flare_model`'s own precedent for a deliberately simplified
          physical model. A full end-to-end numeric reproduction of
          GW170817's own exact published two/three-component fit was NOT
          attempted (would need this session to re-derive Villar 2017's
          precise fitted parameter table); literature-recalled-not-
          re-verified masses land the model a few magnitudes brighter than
          GW170817's real observed peak, most plausibly an amplified
          effect of an imprecise mass recollection given the model's own
          confirmed steep mass-luminosity scaling -- stated as a real,
          open, unresolved gap, not silently accepted as correct.

          `kilonova_eval.py` reuses `gw.py`'s `skymap_path`/`build_skymap_
          from_samples` UNCHANGED for sky localization; distance posterior
          samples are read by a small LOCAL helper in `kilonova_eval.py`
          (not added to `gw.py`, whose own stated scope is sky-
          localization only) from the SAME posterior HDF5 file `gw.
          skymap_path` already downloads. `posterior_coverage`/`sbc_ranks`/
          `CoverageTrial`/`parameter_bias` are reused from `microlensing_
          eval.py` UNCHANGED -- the third roadmap item this session to
          prove that machinery generalizes to a physically unrelated model
          (microlensing, then line-profile fitting, now a kilonova
          apparent-magnitude posterior) with zero changes to
          `microlensing_eval.py` itself. A real performance choice, not
          just a convenience one: flux scales EXACTLY as `1/distance^2`
          for fixed ejecta parameters, so every Monte Carlo trial rescales
          ONE expensive `bolometric_luminosity` evaluation per (parameters,
          epoch) algebraically rather than re-running the quadrature per
          candidate distance -- the naive per-distance approach was
          estimated (from a single quad call's real measured wall-clock
          cost this session) to make a 200-trial x 500-resample coverage
          study take on the order of hours; the rescaling approach is
          exact (not an approximation) and takes seconds.

          `surveys/alerce.py` was live-verified this session for the first
          time since it was added: its own module docstring had flagged
          the response shape as never confirmed against the real service.
          `GET /ztf/v1/objects` (no trailing slash) 302-redirects to the
          trailing-slash form -- `requests` follows this by default, one
          extra round trip, not a correctness bug -- and both `cone_search`
          (a real `{"items": [...]}`-wrapped response) and
          `fetch_light_curves` (a real bare-list response) matched this
          module's existing field-name assumptions exactly; NO bug found
          there. `survey="lsst"` remains unverified. ATLAS forced
          photometry (`fallingstar-data.com/forcedphot/`) was checked live
          and found to be a full account-gated Django dashboard app, not a
          public JSON REST API -- consistent with the plan's fallback,
          marked `[SCOPED]`-pending-credentials (the same "dormant until a
          real data-rights token exists" treatment `rubin_tap.py` already
          has), not built against blind. Real-time DECam GW-EM ToO data has
          no known public catalog at all -- also `[SCOPED]`, same
          reasoning as ASKAP/CRAFT/MeerKAT/NICER in the prior batch. The
          core model/eval do not depend on either, so this did not block
          the item.

          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or `evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_kilonova.py` (26 cases: parameter validation,
          diffusion-timescale scaling with mass/opacity/velocity, heating-
          rate decay and linear mass-scaling and the literature per-gram
          scale check, thermalization-efficiency shape, bolometric-
          luminosity finiteness/positivity/the realistic-peak-scale check/
          the super-linear mass-scaling regression case, photospheric
          temperature-floor and radius expansion/recession behaviour, band
          flux positivity and inverse-square distance scaling, an AB-
          magnitude round-trip check, multi-component summation, and a
          static `rpc.py`-non-reference check), `tests/test_kilonova_eval.py`
          (15 cases: exact flux-rescaling correctness, recall bounded in
          [0,1], the full-footprint and no-signal regression cases, recall
          monotonicity in survey depth and pointing count, and distance-
          conditioned coverage calibration), and `tests/
          test_surveys_alerce.py` (extended with 2 new `live`-marked cases
          confirming the cone-search and detections findings above).


[PARTIAL] Radio variability and FRB-afterglow characterization (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-25
          Fourth item of the next external-roadmap-table batch (items
          21-25), taken after item 21 by user choice as the lower new-
          connector-risk of the two remaining (22/23), since `frb.py`'s
          real CHIME/FRB catalog was already available unchanged.
          `radio_variability.py` (scintillation + synchrotron models) and
          `radio_variability_eval.py` (cross-survey association recall +
          spectral-index uncertainty, split purely for the 500-line
          guideline) are genuinely greenfield -- no scintillation,
          scattering, or synchrotron-fitting code existed anywhere in this
          codebase before this session.

          The plan for this item assumed a CADC/CIRADA-hosted TAP endpoint
          for VLASS, mirroring `frb.py`'s CADC precedent. Checked live this
          session and it did NOT pan out cleanly: CADC's own documented TAP
          endpoint (real subdomain confirmed to be `ws.cadc-ccda.hia-iha.
          nrc-cnrc.gc.ca`, not the `www.` subdomain naively guessed first --
          found only by fetching CADC's own TAP documentation page and
          reading its example script, not by URL-guessing) is real and
          reachable, but has no `vlass`-named table in its `TAP_SCHEMA`;
          CIRADA's own catalogue-documentation PDF (fetched live, 11.6 MB,
          real) could not be parsed with any text-extraction tool available
          in this environment (no `pdftoppm`/`pypdf`/`pymupdf`). What DID
          work, found via a plain web search rather than continued URL-
          guessing: VizieR (CDS) hosts the real "VLASS QL Ep.1 Catalog,
          CIRADA version" (Gordon et al. 2021, ApJS 255, 30) as
          `J/ApJS/255/30/comp`, reachable through the standard, public,
          credential-free IVOA Simple Cone Search protocol -- confirmed
          live with a real cone search returning real `CompName`/
          `RAJ2000`/`DEJ2000`/`Ftot`/`Fpeak`/`DupFlag`/`QualFlag` rows.
          `surveys/vlass.py` targets VizieR instead of CADC/CIRADA directly
          for this reason, reusing `tap.parse_votable` UNCHANGED for the
          response parsing (SCS returns the same VOTable shape TAP's own
          `votable` format does) rather than writing a second XML-table
          parser in this codebase. This is VLASS EPOCH 1 (Quick Look) only;
          a real Epoch 2 catalogue exists per NRAO's own news page, but its
          VizieR identifier was not found this session -- a real, stated
          [GAP], not guessed at; multi-epoch VLASS variability is therefore
          not reachable through this connector yet, unlike the plan's
          original "VLASS epochs" framing for the spectral-index metric.

          The spectral-index metric was adapted to a REAL, better-grounded
          two-point measurement instead: VizieR also hosts NVSS (1.4 GHz,
          `VIII/65/nvss`, confirmed live with real `S1.4`/`e_S1.4` flux
          columns), and VLASS's own catalogue carries an `NVSSdist` nearest-
          neighbour column pointing at exactly this cross-match. `surveys/
          vlass.py`'s new `query_nvss_flux_1_4ghz` plus `radio_variability_
          eval.real_two_point_spectral_index` were run end-to-end against
          REAL data this session (not just mocked responses): a real VLASS
          source near RA=180, Dec=0 with a close real NVSS counterpart
          (`nvss_dist_arcsec` < 5") gave a real spectral index of
          alpha=-1.24 +/- 0.08 (68%) -- a physically sensible steep-
          spectrum synchrotron value, now locked in as a `live`-marked
          regression test. Uncertainty is Monte Carlo error propagation
          (perturb each real flux point within its own reported error and
          refit `radio_variability.fit_spectral_index`), deliberately NOT
          row-resampling bootstrap: a real VLASS+NVSS measurement is
          typically only 2 frequency points, far too few for row-resampling
          to be meaningful, but well suited to propagating each point's own
          Gaussian flux uncertainty through the fit many times.

          A real, load-bearing bug was found via the FULL test suite (not
          this item's own tests, none of which called the failing code
          path): `SurveyConnector.describe()` (`surveys/base.py`) reads
          `self.release` unconditionally on every registered connector, but
          `VLASSConnector` originally exposed only `self.catalog` -- raising
          `AttributeError` the instant `surveys.describe_all()` (called by
          `readiness.status()`) iterated over the newly-registered
          connector, breaking three `test_readiness.py` tests and one
          `test_connectors.py` test that have nothing to do with radio
          astronomy. Fixed by renaming the attribute to `self.release`,
          matching every other connector's convention exactly (`des.py`/
          `sdss.py`/`desi.py` all already use `self.release`) -- a real
          reminder that this codebase's connector contract has a
          cross-cutting requirement (`describe()`) that a new connector's
          own test file, however thorough, will not catch on its own; only
          the full suite does.

          Scintillation: `pulse_broadening_time_ms` implements Bhat et al.
          2004's (ApJ 605, 759) empirical pulse-broadening relation,
          verified this session against the paper's own quoted formula
          (via web search, not memory alone) rather than assumed;
          `decorrelation_bandwidth_mhz` follows from the standard, well-
          established Fourier relation between scattering time and
          frequency coherence bandwidth (Cordes & Rickett 1998). A
          diffractive/refractive scintillation TIMESCALE was deliberately
          NOT implemented: that relation needs an effective scattering-
          screen distance and transverse velocity, which in turn need a
          Galactic electron-density/turbulence model (NE2001 or YMW16) --
          no such model exists anywhere in this codebase (grepped, zero
          hits), and fabricating a screen-distance constant to force a
          timescale number out would have produced an unverified, uncited
          result. Stated as a real, open [GAP] rather than guessed at, the
          same discipline `kilonova.py`'s Barnes-2016-table gap already
          used. Synchrotron: `synchrotron_flux_mjy` is standard textbook
          optically-thin-power-law-plus-self-absorption-turnover physics
          (Rybicki & Lightman 1979 ch. 6), not attributed to a single paper.

          **ASKAP/CRAFT and MeerKAT are `[SCOPED]`**, unchanged from the
          plan: both proposal-gated/proprietary survey programs with no
          known public, credential-free catalogue of individual detections,
          same reasoning as Rubin TAP/ATLAS/DECam in the prior two batches.

          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or `evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_radio_variability.py` (22 cases: pulse-
          broadening/decorrelation-bandwidth scaling with DM and frequency
          and their Fourier-reciprocal relationship, synchrotron parameter
          validation and turnover/self-absorption shape, spectral-index
          recovery on noiseless synthetic points including a closed-form
          two-point check, and a static `rpc.py`-non-reference check),
          `tests/test_radio_variability_eval.py` (12 cases: nearest-
          counterpart matching, cross-survey association recall including
          the perfect-recovery and explicit no-signal regression cases,
          Monte Carlo spectral-index interval reporting and error-width
          scaling, and one `live`-marked end-to-end case against real
          VLASS+NVSS data), and `tests/test_surveys_vlass.py` (14 cases:
          VOTable-based cone-search parsing, the `describe()` regression
          case for the bug above, the NVSS cross-match helper, and 2
          `live`-marked cases confirming real VLASS and NVSS access).


[PARTIAL] X-ray hardness-state modeling (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-25
          Fifth and LAST item of the "next" external-roadmap-table batch
          (items 21-25) -- this batch is now complete: 24, 25, 21, 22, 23
          all landed this multi-session run, matching items 16-20's own
          precedent. `xray_hardness.py` (hardness-ratio computation +
          discrete-state Gaussian-mixture model) and `xray_hardness_eval.py`
          (state-transition detection + flux/hardness calibration, split
          purely for the 500-line guideline) are genuinely greenfield -- no
          hardness-ratio or X-ray-state-classification code existed
          anywhere in this codebase before this session.

          Confirmed this session by reading `chandra.py`/`swift.py`/
          `xmm.py` in full: none of the three existing X-ray connectors
          exposed separate soft/hard band flux at all (`flux_aper_b`,
          `rate`, `ep_flux` respectively -- one broad/total number each),
          so real soft/hard data had to be found from scratch, not just
          wired together. Following this batch's now-established VizieR
          pivot pattern (`vlass.py`/`desi.py`'s own precedent this
          session): Chandra CSC 2.1, 4XMM-DR13, and 2SXPS are ALL real and
          live on VizieR (`IX/70`, `IX/69`, `IX/58` respectively, found via
          web search after Chandra's own `csccli/browse` endpoint was
          confirmed live to need an undocumented `packageset` parameter no
          guessed value resolved), each with genuinely richer data than
          hoped: Chandra 2.1 publishes real per-band fluxes
          (`Fluxb`/`Fluxh`/`Fluxm`/`Fluxs`/`Fluxu`/`Fluxw`) AND its own
          pre-computed `HRhm`/`HRhs`/`HRms`; 4XMM-DR13 and 2SXPS each
          publish pre-computed hardness ratios (`HR1`-`HR4`, `HR1`/`HR2`
          respectively) though not separate per-band flux in their default
          VizieR columns (a real, stated [GAP]). One new additive query
          function was added to each of `chandra.py` (`query_band_
          fluxes`), `xmm.py`, and `swift.py` (both `query_hardness_
          ratios`) -- `cone_search` itself unchanged in all three, the same
          `sdss.py`-precedent pattern this whole batch has followed.
          `surveys/erosita.py` is a new connector (eRASS1, VizieR
          `J/A+A/682/A34`, Merloni et al. 2024, confirmed live).

          A real, quantitative finding from running `flux_hardness_
          calibration` against 76 real CSC 2.1 sources near M87: the
          residual between an independently computed `(Fluxh-Fluxs)/
          (Fluxh+Fluxs)` and CSC's own released `HRhs` is NOT small
          (median ~0.47, robust scatter ~0.34) -- checked live and
          confirmed NOT a formula bug (CSC's own documentation states the
          identical `(F_x-F_y)/(F_x+F_y)` functional form this module
          already implements), but a genuine, informative gap: CSC 2's
          released HR is computed from full photon-flux aperture-
          photometry MPDFs (Bayesian posterior distributions per band),
          "an improvement over CSC Release 1", not from the simple point-
          estimate flux columns this function necessarily uses. This real
          number is now a `live`-marked regression test, not silently
          treated as "should be zero".

          Two more real, live-caught mistakes, both fixed and now locked
          in as regression tests: (1) Chandra/XMM/Swift are POINTED
          observatories with genuinely patchy sky coverage, unlike the
          all-sky VLASS/eROSITA this batch also added -- an arbitrary
          test position (RA=180, Dec=0) legitimately returned zero rows
          from all three, not a bug; fixed by querying M87 (RA=187.7059,
          Dec=12.3911), one of the most Chandra/XMM/Swift-observed targets
          in the sky, in every live test instead. (2) VizieR's `4XMM`
          column holds only the coordinate-designation SUFFIX (e.g.
          "J123049.2+122330"), not a "4XMM "-prefixed full name -- this
          session's own first-draft live test asserted the wrong prefix
          and was caught and corrected by actually running it, not assumed
          correct because it looked plausible.

          `fit_hardness_states` uses `sklearn.mixture.GaussianMixture`
          (`scikit-learn` already a core dependency -- no new package) for
          a genuine K-state classification with a fitted mean/std per
          state, relabelled in ascending-hardness order so "state 0" is
          always the softest state rather than an arbitrary, run-to-run-
          unstable `sklearn` cluster index. `evaluate_false_positive_rate`
          is the explicit no-signal regression case this batch's every
          prior item has included, and reports a genuinely non-zero rate
          honestly (a 2-state mixture fit to pure single-cluster noise
          still partitions into two clusters and can flip labels near
          their boundary -- a real, stated limitation, not hidden).

          **NICER is `[SCOPED]`**, unchanged from the plan: pointed/
          proposal-based with no public all-sky catalogue of hardness
          ratios (unlike Chandra CSC/4XMM-DR13/2SXPS/eRASS1's source
          catalogues), same reasoning as ASKAP/CRAFT/MeerKAT/ATLAS/DECam/
          Rubin-TAP in the prior batches this session.

          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or `evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_xray_hardness.py` (16 cases: hardness-ratio
          edge cases including the zero-total-flux NaN case, discrete-
          state recovery on well-separated synthetic clusters, the
          missing-data-labelled-minus-one case, and transition detection
          including the across-a-gap case), `tests/test_xray_hardness_
          eval.py` (11 cases: detection-rate/false-positive-rate behaviour
          and the flux/hardness residual arithmetic, plus one `live`-
          marked end-to-end case against real CSC data), `tests/
          test_surveys_erosita.py` (10 cases including a `describe()`
          regression case -- added directly this time, learning from the
          VLASS `describe()` bug in the prior item -- and one `live` case),
          and `tests/test_surveys_chandra.py`/`test_surveys_xmm.py`/
          `test_surveys_swift.py` each extended with a new `TestQuery*`
          class (parsing, provider, and one `live`-marked case) for their
          new additive query function.

          **This closes out the 21-25 batch.** Full suite: 1776 passed,
          23 skipped, 0 failed.


[PARTIAL] Physics-informed stellar latent manifold (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-22
          `stellar_manifold.py` compares a star's real, extinction-corrected
          Gaia CMD position (BP-RP colour, absolute G magnitude) against a
          real, published main-sequence track and reports the residual --
          this project's answer to "residuals to isochrones". `ZAMS_TRACK`
          is a real, live-verified subset of Eric Mamajek's maintained
          dwarf-star table (Pecaut & Mamajek 2013, ApJS 208, 9;
          EEM_dwarf_UBVIJHK_colors_Teff.txt, fetched 2026-08-22), B9V
          (10700 K) through M7V (2680 K) -- the O/B0-B7 end of that table
          does not publish a Gaia BP-RP column, so the track deliberately
          starts at B9V; an honest scope limit, not a gap glossed over. ONE
          static, roughly solar-metallicity track, not a fitted multi-age/
          multi-metallicity isochrone grid -- a giant, subdwarf, or strongly
          non-solar-metallicity star is expected to show a real, physically
          meaningful residual against it, not necessarily an error.
          `nearest_track_point`/`isochrone_residual` report a VERTICAL
          residual at fixed colour (observed M_G minus the track's own M_G
          at that BP-RP), the standard "how many magnitudes off the main
          sequence" convention, not a mixed-unit Euclidean nearest point.
          Extinction correction uses two DISTINCT real Gaia quantities: `a_g`
          (G-band extinction, corrects the magnitude) and Gaia's own
          `ebpminrp_val` (E(BP-RP), corrects the colour directly) -- a
          different, more direct convention than `scoring.
          physical_inconsistency`'s existing `2.74 * ebv` fallback, which
          only ever corrects a magnitude and was no precedent for colour.
          `featurematrix.join_stellar_manifold_columns` appends
          `manifold_residual_mag`/`manifold_arc_length`/`manifold_teff_k`/
          `manifold_matched` onto a matrix that already carries
          `GAIA_JOIN_COLUMNS`. `join_gaia_columns` itself now also attaches
          `gaia_a_g`/`gaia_ebpminrp` (already computed by
          `derived_properties()` but not previously surfaced) -- as IDENTITY
          DICT keys, deliberately NOT feature-matrix value columns. A first
          version added them as columns and broke `test_ablation.py`'s
          `ztf_gaia` scorability test: extinction is frequently absent even
          for an otherwise well-matched source, and `finite_mask()` excludes
          a row if ANY column is non-finite, so a matched row with no
          extinction was silently excluded from every OTHER detector too --
          exactly the kind of one feature silently disabling an unrelated
          one this project's NaN-not-imputed convention exists to prevent.
          Living in the identity dict (plain per-row metadata, never
          inspected by `finite_mask()`) avoids that; caught by the existing
          ablation regression test before this shipped, not after.
          `surveys/sdss.py` gained real spectrum acquisition, and a real bug
          was found and fixed along the way: `cone_search()` previously
          selected `plate, mjd, fiberID` `FROM PhotoObj` -- confirmed live
          against the real SkyServer SQL endpoint that this returns HTTP 500
          (those columns live on `SpecObjAll`, not `PhotoObj`). Fixed to
          query `SpecObjAll` for `bestObjID, specObjID, plate, mjd, fiberID,
          run2d, class` (falling back to `specObjID` when `bestObjID` is
          `0` -- confirmed live this happens for spectra with no matched
          photometric counterpart, rather than dropping the row).
          `fetch_spectrum` downloads the real, live-verified, public,
          unauthenticated spec-lite FITS URL
          (data.sdss.org/sas/dr17/sdss/spectro/redux/{run2d}/spectra/lite/
          {plate:04d}/spec-{plate:04d}-{mjd:05d}-{fiber:04d}.fits --
          confirmed by downloading a real 168.8 KB file and inspecting it
          with astropy) via `netclient.download`, the same bounded/atomic/
          SHA-256 primitive the TESS/ZTF product pipelines already use.
          `extract_sdss_spectrum` does NOT reuse `spectral_features.
          from_fits()`'s generic column matcher: SDSS's `COADD` table stores
          `loglam` (log10-Angstrom), not a `wavelength`/`wave`/`lambda`
          column, confirmed live -- this function converts `loglam` itself,
          drops `and_mask`-flagged bad pixels, then calls the existing,
          unmodified `spectral_features.extract()`. The same real file
          verified while building this also carries a `SPECOBJ` row with
          SDSS's OWN legacy ELODIE cross-correlation stellar parameters (a
          real, non-fabricated `CLASS=STAR, ELODIE_TEFF=6890.0 K,
          ELODIE_LOGG=4.184, ELODIE_FEH=-1.62` for the specific object
          checked) -- real spectroscopic evidence this connector surfaces,
          not a new ASTRA fit. Populated only for SEGUE-era stellar spectra
          and frequently absent; a non-finite or non-positive `ELODIE_TEFF`
          is treated as "not available", never a fabricated 0 K.
          `compare_to_spectroscopic_teff` is the genuine use of that real
          spectroscopy: the fractional discrepancy between the CMD-implied
          Teff and the real ELODIE one, a cross-check that can flag a blend
          or misclassified spectrum.
          For "improvement in anomaly precision at fixed recall",
          `stellar_manifold_eval.py` had to build TWO pieces of general
          infrastructure this codebase did not already have: direct
          feature-space injection (`inject_cmd_outliers` -- `evaluate.
          build_injected()` only ever injects into light-curve sequences and
          recomputes features afterward; recomputes `manifold_residual_mag`
          for injected rows too, or the measured "improvement" would be an
          artefact of a stale feature) and precision at a FIXED RECALL level
          (`precision_at_recall`, via `sklearn.metrics.
          precision_recall_curve` -- `evaluate.score_method`'s precision@k
          is at a fixed top-k COUNT and `ranker.metrics` is at a fixed 0.5
          THRESHOLD; neither is this operating-point convention).
          `evaluate_manifold_contribution` reuses the real, unmodified
          `anomaly.detect()` ensemble -- new evidence and a new evaluation,
          not a new detector -- and restricts BOTH arms (with/without the
          manifold feature) to the exact same fully Gaia- and manifold-
          matched, fully finite row population before injecting, so a
          reported difference cannot be an artefact of the two arms scoring
          different objects (the same "different populations" trap
          `join_gaia_columns`'s own docstring already warns about, citing
          the ztf_gaia entry above). It NEVER asserts a winner, only reports
          both arms' precision at each requested recall with a multi-seed
          mean/CI95 summary -- the same restraint `sweep.SweepResult.best()`
          already applies elsewhere in this codebase.
          Explicitly NOT attempted: a fitted multi-age/multi-metallicity
          isochrone grid (PARSEC/MIST via VizieR remains open -- `astroquery.
          vizier` is already a dependency, used today only for VSX in
          catalogs.py); a giant-branch or subdwarf track; running
          `evaluate_manifold_contribution` at Stage-B scale on real matched
          ZTF/Gaia/SDSS populations (built and tested on synthetic data
          only, the same "mechanism validated, not yet run at scale" caveat
          this file already carries for the hierarchical multiband GP model
          and the hyperparameter sweep). Like every other opt-in research
          module in this file, none of this is wired into `rpc.py`,
          `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or `evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_stellar_manifold.py` (19 cases), `tests/
          test_stellar_manifold_eval.py` (10 cases), `tests/test_anomaly.py`
          (`TestStellarManifoldJoin`, 3 cases), and `tests/
          test_surveys_sdss.py` (extended to 19 cases: the `SpecObjAll`
          fix, `bestObjID=0` fallback, spectrum URL/download, and the
          `loglam`/`and_mask`/`ELODIE_TEFF` adapter).


[PARTIAL] Detector scores have empirical calibration metadata
          A score of 1.0 means "most anomalous in THIS batch", not an
          absolute level. Rankings from different runs are not directly
          comparable. The ensemble now also exposes empirical-CDF calibration
          metadata and records whether it is batch-relative or backed by an
          external reference population.

PHASE 5 — DEEP LEARNING

[PARTIAL] Absolute-flux-aware multimodal contrastive encoder (P1) (section 14)  <-- NEW 2026-08-23
          The blocker stated below (kept, struck through in spirit, for the
          record) is now resolved by MoCo-style momentum contrast (He et
          al. 2019, "Momentum Contrast for Unsupervised Visual
          Representation Learning"), not by more VRAM: a momentum-updated
          (EMA, m=0.999) copy of each modality's encoder produces "key"
          embeddings pushed onto a FIFO queue (`multimodal_moco.
          EmbeddingQueue`, default 2048), so the number of negatives is a
          memory knob on the queue, completely decoupled from the
          forward/backward batch size that gradient accumulation could
          never touch. This is a genuine mechanism resolution, not a
          workaround -- the original blocker's own reasoning
          ("gradient accumulation gives the loss no additional negatives")
          is exactly the thing MoCo's queue supplies instead.
          Original blocking note, preserved: "The stated central research
          innovation. Contrastive objectives get their signal from
          in-batch negatives and want batches of 1024-4096; ~2.2 GB usable
          VRAM allows perhaps 64-128. Gradient accumulation does NOT fix
          this -- it accumulates gradients but gives the loss no additional
          negatives."

          Four modality branches (`multimodal_encoders.py`): a new small
          Conv2d image CNN (~27K params -- the FIRST Conv2d/2-D image model
          anywhere in this codebase; every other model here is Conv1d over
          sequences), a new patch-embedding transformer spectrum encoder
          mirroring `pretrain.make_encoder`'s exact shape but 3 input
          channels (log-wavelength, flux, error) instead of 2, the
          light-curve branch reused VERBATIM from backlog item 13
          (`pretrain.make_encoder`/`load_pretrained_encoder`, zero
          reimplementation), and a small catalog-metadata MLP over
          `features.FEATURE_NAMES` + `featurematrix.GAIA_JOIN_COLUMNS`.
          Checked VRAM arithmetic (same measured-not-assumed discipline as
          the `[KNOWN] Model capacity` entry below): on the order of 250K
          trainable parameters across all four branches, well under 50 MB
          activation memory at batch 64 -- two orders of magnitude below
          the 2.2 GB budget. Model capacity was never the real constraint;
          the queue is what actually resolves this entry's blocker.

          Topology: light curve is the contrastive ANCHOR, not full
          pairwise C(4,2)=6. Three symmetric pairs (lightcurve-image,
          lightcurve-spectrum, lightcurve-catalog; 6 InfoNCE directions, 4
          FIFO queues, one shared by all 3 pairs' light-curve keys) --
          justified by data scarcity: an object needing two NON-anchor
          modalities simultaneously at once is a rarer real intersection
          than "has a light curve plus one other modality," and light-curve
          ingestion is this codebase's base survey product.

          A real per-modality "physical scale token" (`multimodal_encoders.
          signed_log_scale` of a real absolute-scale scalar -- `features.py`'s
          `mean` for light curves, a fixed synthetic peak/continuum-scale
          proxy in validation data) is fused into each modality's pooled
          embedding BEFORE the shared contrastive projection head
          (`fuse_scale`/`encode_and_fuse`), directly answering the
          `[KNOWN] Sequence representation discards absolute brightness`
          entry below ("a model that needs it must consume both"). This
          makes "brightness-preservation error" concrete: `multimodal_eval.
          probe_brightness_preservation` trains a frozen probe to regress
          the real scalar back out of the fused embedding and reports RMSE
          in real physical units, not normalised-space MSE.

          A new bounded raw-pixel-array loader was required first: NO
          existing public function returned a raw pixel array before this
          work. `image_features.extract()` and `fitsio.image_payload()`
          both load real float arrays internally but only ever returned
          scalar summaries or a lossy 8-bit display quantization, and
          `tess_pixels._read_tpf`'s real per-cadence flux cube was private
          and discarded by its only caller (`extract_photometry`, which is
          unchanged). `fitsio.raw_pixel_array()` (reuses `decimate()`,
          returns the bounded float array before zscale/8-bit quantization)
          and `tess_pixels.read_tpf_cube()` (a thin public export of
          `_read_tpf`) now expose those arrays; `multimodal_pixels.py`
          bounds/reduces/fixed-sizes both surveys' cutouts into one common
          32x32 CNN input shape.

          Two real numerical bugs were found and fixed while building this,
          both worth recording since they'd silently recur in similar code:
          (1) `EmbeddingQueue.negatives()` originally returned a VIEW into
          its ring buffer, not a copy -- since every pair's forward pass
          reads one queue's negatives and then immediately enqueues into
          that SAME queue before the next pair runs, the later enqueue's
          in-place write corrupted a tensor already captured in the current
          step's autograd graph, raising "modified by an inplace operation"
          on backward. Fixed by cloning in `negatives()`. (2) Feeding raw,
          orders-of-magnitude-varying flux directly into the untrained
          image/spectrum encoders produced NaN activations under float16
          autocast on this machine's real GPU (a GTX 1650) within the first
          training epoch -- confirming, not just assuming, that this
          codebase's own light-curve precedent (`tensors.normalise()`
          discarding absolute brightness by design) is the right
          convention for every modality, not an optional nicety: image and
          spectrum encoder inputs are now unit-scale shape-only arrays,
          with the real scalar carried entirely by the scale token.
          `info_nce_loss` additionally computes in float32 regardless of
          autocast state, the same "numerically sensitive step forced to
          float32 under AMP" discipline `models.ConvVAE.reparameterise`
          already applies for the identical Turing-has-no-bfloat16 reason.

          Validated on SYNTHETIC paired multimodal data
          (`multimodal_synthetic.build_synthetic_pairs`): checked this
          session that no connector in this codebase (including
          `alerce.py`) assembles a real object with an image cutout AND a
          spectrum AND a light curve AND a Gaia match all downloaded at
          once -- real multi-modal-complete acquisition remains an
          explicit, stated-as-open next step, the same "mechanism validated
          on synthetic ground truth, not yet run at real Stage-B scale"
          caveat `multiband_hier.py`/`moving_objects.py`/
          `stellar_manifold_eval.py` already carry. Every synthetic
          object's four modality-scale scalars are derived from ONE shared
          magnitude via the real `flux = 10**(-0.4*(mag-zeropoint))`
          relation, so they are genuinely correlated the way a real
          object's would be, not four independently-randomised numbers.

          Metrics: linear-probe macro-F1 (frozen projection embedding ->
          `LogisticRegression` on synthetic `CLASS_KINDS`,
          `f1_score(average="macro")`, same frozen-encoder-plus-sklearn-probe
          pattern as `pretrain_probe.probe_transfer` generalised to
          multiclass); cross-survey retrieval recall@{1,5,10}
          (`multimodal_eval.retrieval_recall`, cosine nearest-neighbor of
          L2-normalised projection outputs against known synthetic
          object-id pairing); brightness-preservation error (above).

          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or `evidence.py`.

          Tests: `tests/test_multimodal_pixels.py` (12 cases),
          `tests/test_multimodal_encoders.py` (19 cases),
          `tests/test_multimodal_synthetic.py` (8 cases),
          `tests/test_multimodal_moco.py` (21 cases, including explicit
          regression tests for both numerical bugs above), and
          `tests/test_multimodal_eval.py` (8 cases).

[PARTIAL] Masked self-supervised light-curve pretraining (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-22
          `pretrain.py` (encoder + pretraining loop) and `pretrain_probe.py`
          (transfer evaluation, split into its own file purely to keep each
          under this project's 500-line guideline) are new, self-contained
          research modules: a
          patch-embedding transformer encoder (`make_encoder`, deliberately
          mirroring `models.make_transformer`'s Conv1d patch-embed shape so
          results are comparable) trained on TWO per-sample pretext
          objectives over unlabeled 2-channel (value, gap-mask) sequences --
          neither uses labels of any kind. Unlike the `[BLOCKED]` multimodal/
          contrastive entry immediately above, this is explicitly NOT a
          contrastive objective: it needs no in-batch negatives and no
          1024-4096 batch, so the ~2.2 GB VRAM budget that blocks section 14
          does not block this.
          (1) Span-masking reconstruction: additional spans of currently-
          VALID points (tensors.py's own gap-validity mask == 1) are hidden
          and the model must reconstruct just those points. This is
          carefully NOT the same mask as tensors.py's gap-validity channel:
          `apply_span_mask` returns a separate `pretrain_mask`, samples only
          from already-valid positions (hiding an interpolated gap point
          would train the model to reconstruct a fabricated value), and the
          loss (`span_reconstruction_loss`) is restricted to
          pretrain_mask * gap_mask so a gap point is never credited either
          way. A row with fewer than 8 real valid points is skipped, not
          degraded, the same convention `tensors.resample()`'s MIN_POINTS
          cutoff already uses.
          (2) Time-order corruption detection: non-adjacent patch pairs are
          swapped (`make_order_task`, both channels swapped together so a
          shuffled row's gap-mask stays consistent with its own value) and a
          head trained to detect whether a row's patch order was corrupted.
          Adjacent patches are deliberately never swapped -- too weak a
          corruption for a clean label boundary. The two tasks run on
          independently-corrupted copies of the same batch each step and are
          trained jointly through a SHARED encoder.
          Deliberately its own training loop (`pretrain()`), not
          `train.train()`: `train.py`'s `_loss_for` unpacks
          `(prediction, mu, logvar)` against a single masked-MSE-plus-
          optional-KL contract with no slot for a second head/second loss,
          and bending that signature risked every existing
          autoencoder/VAE/transformer/neural_ode caller. `pretrain()`
          instead directly reuses the kind-agnostic pieces of `train.py`
          that ARE reusable: `choose_batch_size`, `_cuda_oom_guard`,
          `_set_seed`, and the same AMP/GradScaler/accumulation/
          early-stopping/checkpoint-on-val-improvement shape.
          Transfer is measured by `pretrain_probe.probe_transfer`: the
          pretrained encoder
          is frozen, pooled embeddings extracted, and a cheap
          `LogisticRegression` head trained at label fractions {1%, 10%,
          100%}, compared at the same fractions against a from-scratch
          baseline (`LogisticRegression` on `evaluate.sequence_summary`) --
          the SAME held-out test partition is reused across every fraction
          (`_train_test_split_indices`), so a result cannot be an artefact
          of an easier test split at one fraction than another. Each
          fraction is repeated 5 times with an independent label subsample
          and reported as mean/std/ci95, the same "report an interval, not a
          single number" discipline `sweep.py`'s CI95-gated `best()` already
          establishes elsewhere in this codebase; a repeat with too few
          labelled rows of one class is skipped and counted, never silently
          scored as NaN.
          As with EVERY OTHER measurement in this codebase (see this file's
          "Supervised evaluation metrics" and `evaluate.py`'s own docstring),
          "labels" here means `evaluate.build_injected`'s true-by-
          construction injection labels, NOT real human annotations --
          Phase 7's human-in-the-loop labelling system does not exist yet,
          so this is the same honest stand-in injection-recovery already
          provides everywhere else, with the same stated caveat: it measures
          transfer to the anomaly TYPES actually injected, not to whatever a
          human would eventually flag.
          Explicitly NOT attempted: no adaptive/curriculum masking ratio, no
          multi-scale patch sizes, no cross-survey (ZTF+TESS pooled)
          pretraining run at scale (the survey-separation bias this file's
          own "Survey/band/tier-stratified detection" entry already
          measured applies here too and is not re-litigated by this
          change), and no attempt to beat the Stage-B `deep_transformer`
          reconstruction-only baseline -- this module answers "does
          pretraining help at low label budgets", a different question from
          "which anomaly-scoring method wins". Like `multiband_hier.py` and
          every other new opt-in research module in this file, `pretrain.py`
          is deliberately NOT wired into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or
          `evidence.py`: new evidence about a training strategy, not a score
          change.
          Tests: `tests/test_pretrain.py` (32 cases): span-masking
          correctness (never hides an already-invalid gap point, gap-mask
          channel left untouched), order-task correctness (channels swapped
          together, adjacent patches never swapped), encoder/model shapes,
          loss correctness (the pretrain_mask * gap_mask composition is
          explicitly tested), the training loop (loss reduction, checkpoint
          round-trip, early stopping, reproducibility, accumulation), probe
          transfer (all three fractions present, fixed test split reused,
          a too-small fraction skips rather than crashes), and a static
          check that `rpc.py` has no reference to this module.
          UPDATE 2026-08-28: added `pretrain_join.py`
          (`join_pretrained_embeddings`), a column-join utility so a
          researcher CAN append a checkpoint's embeddings onto an existing
          `FeatureMatrix` -- modelled on `featurematrix.join_gaia_columns`'s
          proven shape (column join, not row union; NaN for a row whose
          light curve is unreadable/too-short rather than an imputed
          value). This is deliberately NOT a promotion of pretraining into
          live scoring: no checkpoint exists anywhere in this repo, no
          favorable `probe_transfer` result has ever been measured (unlike
          the anomaly ensemble's own rank-consensus decision above, which
          cites a real 0.824-vs-0.840 measurement), and `anomaly.detect()`/
          `rpc.py` are unchanged -- a researcher must call
          `join_pretrained_embeddings` explicitly and separately compare
          detection quality with/without the joined columns (e.g. via
          `research/benchmark.py`/`ablation.py`, both already `FeatureMatrix`-
          agnostic) before drawing any conclusion. Confirmed column-generic
          end-to-end, not just by inspection: `tests/test_pretrain_join.py`
          (5 cases: row count and column names preserved, all-valid-curves
          match rate, an unreadable curve gets NaN not a dropped row, the
          empty-matrix case, and the joined columns passed through
          `anomaly.detect()` without error).

[KNOWN]   Model capacity is far below what the GPU allows  <-- MEASURED
          The autoencoder is 93,921 parameters and peaks at 38 MB of
          VRAM; the VAE is 126,705 parameters at 39 MB. Against ~2.3 GB
          free that is about 1.5% of the budget. The 4 GB card is NOT
          the binding constraint for these models.
          => Capacity should be chosen by the injection-recovery
             comparison, not by the memory limit. Plan section 13's rule
             cuts both ways: bigger is not automatically better either.

[KNOWN]   Sequence representation discards absolute brightness
          Curves are MAD-normalised so ZTF magnitudes and TESS flux are
          comparable in shape. Absolute brightness survives only in the
          feature vector. A model that needs it must consume both.

[KNOWN]   Gaps are masked, not modelled
          Points interpolated across observing gaps are zeroed and
          flagged in a validity channel so they never enter the loss.
          A model that reasons about irregular sampling directly (e.g.
          a neural ODE or a time-aware attention) would use the gaps as
          information rather than discarding them.
          UPDATE 2026-08-22: the neural-ODE side of this is now built.
          `tensors.py` gained a 4th mode, `"irregular"`
          (`resample_irregular`, `RESAMPLE_MODES`): unlike every other mode,
          it does NOT interpolate at all -- it keeps only real observations
          (subsampled by index, never invented, when a curve has more than
          `length` of them) and reports each kept point's real gap since the
          previous one, scaled by the curve's own median native spacing and
          capped at `IRREGULAR_MAX_GAP_FACTOR` (the same 30x threshold
          `season_bounds` already uses to call a gap "a missed observing
          season" rather than ordinary jitter). This produces a 3-channel
          (value, mask, scaled time-delta) batch, one channel more than
          every other mode's 2-channel one; there is no silent fallback to
          the time grid for a curve this mode cannot represent, since that
          would mix a 2-channel row into an otherwise 3-channel batch.
          `models.make_neural_ode` consumes it: an ODE-RNN (Rubanova et al.
          2019) that evolves a hidden state CONTINUOUSLY between
          observations via a small MLP vector field integrated with a
          fixed-step 4th-order Runge-Kutta solver, using each gap's REAL
          duration (not a fixed unit step), then updates the state
          DISCONTINUOUSLY at each real observation via a `nn.GRUCell`; a
          padding step evolves the ODE but skips the GRU jump. Deliberately
          did NOT add `torchdiffeq`/`torchcde` as a new dependency: the real,
          already-capped gap duration needs no adaptive-step solver, so a
          hand-written fixed-step RK4 (`ode_steps` substeps per gap) is both
          sufficient and one fewer gated research dependency than planned.
          Because the model still returns `(prediction, None, None)` and a
          shared linear decoder reconstructs a scalar value at every step,
          `masked_reconstruction_loss`/`train.py`'s `_loss_for`/`train()`
          needed ZERO kind-specific changes -- the existing training loop,
          checkpointing, and OOM guard all apply unchanged. Verified directly
          (not just unit-tested): a tiny hand-run training loop on a
          deterministic synthetic sine wave dropped the masked
          reconstruction loss from 0.47 to 1.6e-5 over 150 steps before any
          formal test was written.
          Wired into the existing extension points exactly as each already
          works: `sweep.py` gained `NEURAL_ODE_GRID` (`ode_hidden_dim`,
          `ode_steps`, `learning_rate`) and a branch in `grid()`, with a
          clear guard (`kind='neural_ode' requires mode='irregular'`) instead
          of letting a mismatched 2-channel batch fail with an opaque
          tensor-shape error deep inside the model; `evaluate._deep_methods`
          now branches on `values.shape[1]` -- 2 channels still runs
          `("autoencoder", "vae", "transformer")`, 3 channels runs only
          `("neural_ode",)`, since the two representations cannot share one
          array. `stageb.run` needed NO changes at all: it already forwards
          `mode` to `tensors.build`, so `stageb.run(mode="irregular",
          include_deep=True)` already exercises the neural ODE through the
          existing checkpointed Stage-B comparison.
          Explicitly NOT yet done: because the two representations are
          shape-incompatible, a single `compare_on_sequences` call still
          only compares the models that share ITS batch's channel count --
          a full four-way "PR-AUC under cadence shift vs. the gap-masking
          baseline" comparison (this feature's own stated success metric)
          means calling `compare_on_sequences` twice, once per
          representation, against labels built by `build_injected` on each,
          and has not itself been run at Stage-B scale yet -- the same
          "built and tested, not yet run at scale" caveat this file already
          carries for the sweep mechanism and the method-comparison harness
          themselves.
          Tests: `tests/test_tensors.py` (10 new cases: 3-channel shape, real
          non-interpolated values, padding, zero first delta, gap detection,
          gap capping, subsampling, no time-grid fallback, build()/split()
          channel-count handling), `tests/test_deep.py` (forward shape,
          gradient flow, shared-loop training, `_deep_methods` dispatch by
          channel count), `tests/test_sweep.py` (own grid dimensions,
          capacity explored both directions, the mode guard). Also fixed
          alongside: `experiment.RESAMPLING_CONTRACTS` had no `"irregular"`
          entry, so its preprocessing-schema hash would have silently
          collided with `"time"`'s (both falling back to the same default
          contract) despite being genuinely different, shape-incompatible
          representations -- caught by the existing "each mode hashes
          differently" test once `"irregular"` joined `RESAMPLE_MODES`.

[KNOWN]   Injection-recovery measures the anomalies that were injected
          The comparison harness scores methods on recovering flares,
          eclipses, step changes and noise bursts. Winning there proves
          sensitivity to THOSE shapes. It does not prove sensitivity to
          phenomena nobody thought to inject, which is the actual
          discovery case in plan section 4.
          => Partially addressed: see "Open-world transient injection
             simulator" below, which measures recovery on real held-out
             transients instead of only the four shapes listed here.

[PARTIAL] Open-world transient injection simulator (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-23
          Answers the `[KNOWN]` entry immediately above with a genuinely
          different comparison, not a bigger version of the same one:
          instead of proving a detector recovers the four hand-designed
          shapes it was tuned to expect, `open_world_eval.
          evaluate_open_world_generalization` asks whether it recovers
          REAL, held-out ALeRCE-classified transients it never saw during
          design -- the actual discovery case plan section 4 names.
          `diffusion.py` is a new, minimal 1-D DDPM (Ho et al. 2020) -- the
          first flow/diffusion/GAN-family model anywhere in this codebase
          (confirmed: zero prior art; the only existing generative model is
          the unrelated, plain reconstruction `models.ConvVAE`). A genuine
          U-Net (down-path activations kept and concatenated back into the
          matching up-path stage, a sinusoidal timestep embedding broadcast
          into every block) -- 63,505 parameters at default config,
          negligible against the VRAM budget the same way every other deep
          model in this file already measures itself. `timesteps` defaults
          to 100, not image-diffusion's usual 1000+: the real cost of a
          diffusion model on this hardware is the ITERATIVE reverse sampler
          (one forward pass per step), not parameter count or per-step
          VRAM, so T is chosen for affordable sampling, deliberately not
          maximum sample fidelity -- a real tradeoff, stated rather than
          hidden. Only the value channel is noised; the gap-validity mask
          is conditioning information the denoiser sees but never
          generates, and `diffusion_loss` scores the noise prediction only
          at real (non-interpolated) points -- the same masking discipline
          `models.masked_reconstruction_loss` already applies everywhere
          else, here applied to a noise target instead of a value target.
          `open_world_injection.py` trains that denoiser on REAL light-
          curve windows (`extract_real_patches`, reusing `tensors.resample`
          unchanged -- no second resampling path written), not synthetic
          curves: the generator learns what real variability shape looks
          like, broader than "anomaly-only," so a sampled patch is not a
          remix of `evaluate.ANOMALY_KINDS`. `inject_generative`/
          `build_injected_open_world` splice a sampled patch onto a REAL
          stored baseline sequence (real cadence, real gaps, real per-point
          noise) -- this is what "real survey noise" concretely means here.
          `build_injected_open_world` produces the exact same
          `InjectionResult` shape `evaluate.build_injected` does, so it is
          handed to the existing, UNMODIFIED `evaluate.compare_on_sequences`
          -- confirmed no fork of that function was needed; it only ever
          consumes an already-built `InjectionResult`.
          `surveys/alerce.py` gained `query_classified_objects()` (a new
          function alongside `cone_search`, not a change to it -- this is a
          classifier-filtered query, not a spatial one), live-verified this
          session: `classifier=lc_classifier&class=SNIa&order_by=
          probability&order_mode=DESC` against the real, confirmed-live
          `/ztf/v1/objects` endpoint returns real classified ZTF supernova
          candidates with populated `oid`/`class`/`classifier`/
          `probability` -- a plain positional cone search on the same
          endpoint returns these fields `null` for most objects; the
          classifier filter is what actually selects classified rows.
          `open_world_eval.assemble_held_out_set` uses this to build a real
          positive set (`fetch_light_curves`, already real, reused
          unchanged) plus negatives drawn from the design population
          itself -- carried with an explicit, unavoidable caveat: those
          negatives are NOT individually verified as non-transient, only
          presumed normal by construction of the query. TNS would give
          higher-quality real labels (`catalogs._fetch_tns`, already real
          and live) but needs a user-supplied API credential not available
          this session; that remains open exactly as the credential
          barrier itself, not a code gap.
          `evaluate_open_world_generalization` trains the SAME architecture
          (a plain reconstruction autoencoder via the existing, unmodified
          `train.train()`/`models.ModelConfig` path `evaluate._deep_methods`
          already uses) two ways -- closed-world (`evaluate.build_injected`)
          and open-world (`build_injected_open_world`) -- and scores BOTH
          on the identical held-out real set via
          `train.reconstruction_scores()` + `evaluate.score_method`,
          multi-seed with a mean/CI95 summary (`sweep.TrialResult._summary`'s
          shape, used throughout this codebase). It NEVER asserts which arm
          wins -- the same restraint `sweep.SweepResult.best()`/
          `stellar_manifold_eval.evaluate_manifold_contribution` already
          apply elsewhere: this is a comparison to report, not a
          conclusion to assume.
          At the time this entry was first written, three gaps were stated
          explicitly rather than glossed over -- real instrumental-artifact
          material, running the study at real scale, and conditional
          generation. All three are now closed; see the entry immediately
          below for what changed and the real measured result.
          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or `evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_diffusion.py` (31 cases, including
          conditioning), `tests/test_open_world_injection.py` (14 cases),
          `tests/test_surveys_alerce.py` (extended, 5 new cases for
          `query_classified_objects`), `tests/test_open_world_eval.py`
          (9 cases), `tests/test_artifact_patches.py` (18 cases),
          `tests/test_open_world_scale.py` (6 cases), `tests/
          test_tess_pixels.py` (extended, 6 new cases for `find_sectors`).

[PARTIAL] Open-world simulator: real artifacts, scale run, conditioning (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-23
          Closes the three gaps the entry above stated explicitly. All
          three were re-examined rather than assumed still blocked, and two
          turned out to be genuinely buildable with data this codebase
          already has real access to.
          (1) Real instrumental artifacts. Previously called blocked; that
          was an underclaim. Real per-cadence TESS `QUALITY` bitmasks
          (cosmic rays, stray light, coarse pointing, desaturation...) are
          real and available -- but they never reach disk: confirmed by
          reading both files that `tess_pixels.persist_photometry` drops
          the quality array when building the `LightCurve` it persists, and
          `store.SCHEMA` is strictly `time`/`value`/`value_err` with no
          per-point flag column. Changing that schema (plus `LightCurve`,
          `_with_points`, `to_arrays`) to carry one research module's flag
          data was judged not worth touching this codebase's most
          load-bearing files for -- deliberately NOT done.
          `artifact_patches.py` reads the raw TPF instead, via the public
          `tess_pixels.read_tpf_cube()` wrapper (already added this
          session): real per-cadence `flux` cube + real `quality` array,
          straight from a real downloaded product, no schema change needed.
          Category names and bit values (`cosmic_ray`, `stray_light`,
          `pointing`, `systematic`, `excluded`) come from
          `lightkurve.utils.TessQualityFlags` -- already a core dependency,
          a real authoritative table, not hand-recalled constants.
          `extract_artifact_patches` slices fixed-length windows centred on
          real runs of flagged cadences (plus real clean contrast windows),
          MAD-normalised via the existing `tensors.normalise`.
          Since no TPF FITS files existed locally, `tess_pixels.py` gained
          `find_sectors()` (a new function, the real live MAST TESScut
          sector-lookup endpoint, confirmed live this session) and
          `download_reference_tpfs()` wraps it with the existing real
          `download_tpf` to acquire a small number of real TPFs.
          Verified against a REAL download, not just a synthetic fixture:
          fetched a real TPF (RA 219.9, Dec -60.8, sector 11) and found
          103 real flagged cadences out of 1248 (systematic, excluded,
          stray_light categories all present with real bit values),
          extracting 12 real patches (6 clean, 4 excluded, 2 stray_light).
          ZTF artifacts remain genuinely unavailable, stated plainly:
          `surveys/ztf.py` sends `BAD_CATFLAGS_MASK=32768` on every
          request, so IRSA strips flagged epochs server-side before they
          ever reach this codebase -- recovering them means changing
          acquisition behaviour for the whole ZTF pipeline, out of scope
          for one research module.
          (2) Conditional generation. `diffusion.py`'s `DiffusionConfig`
          gained `n_artifact_classes`/`n_transient_classes` (0 = disabled).
          `make_denoiser` gains two INDEPENDENT `nn.Embedding` tables when
          enabled (each with an extra "unspecified" index), added into the
          shared timestep embedding before it's broadcast into every block
          -- the same "fuse a real condition into a shared embedding"
          pattern `multimodal_encoders.encode_and_fuse` already established
          this session. A patch can carry an artifact category, a real
          ALeRCE transient class, both, or neither. With both counts at 0
          the model is structurally IDENTICAL to the unconditional version
          -- verified: every one of the original 22 unconditional
          `test_diffusion.py` cases still passes unchanged.
          `diffusion.py` (529 lines with the training loop inline) was
          split into `diffusion.py` (architecture: config, schedule,
          `make_denoiser`, loss) and `diffusion_train.py` (training loop,
          `sample()`, checkpoint I/O) purely to stay under this project's
          500-line guideline -- the same split `pretrain.py`/
          `pretrain_probe.py` already went through earlier this session.
          (3) Scale run. `open_world_scale.py`'s `run_scale_study` is a
          resumable runner (checkpointed per stage: held-out set assembly,
          generator training, the final comparison), the same discipline
          `stageb.py`'s `run()` already establishes for a real run against
          a live external service that must survive interruption.
          ACTUALLY EXECUTED against real data this session (not just
          tested): real ALeRCE SNIa candidates, real locally-stored ZTF
          light curves as the design population and negatives, a real
          diffusion generator trained on real patches. Completed in 21.8s.
          Result, reported honestly rather than cherry-picked: only 3 real
          ALeRCE SNIa candidates cleared the default probability floor and
          fetched successfully in this small run, giving a held-out set of
          6 rows (3 positive, 3 negative) -- genuinely modest, stated as
          such. Both arms (closed-world hand-designed injection,
          open-world generative injection) scored IDENTICALLY
          (ROC-AUC mean 0.7407, std 0.0642, 3 seeds) at this sample size --
          checked this is not a bug (the two injection schemes were
          separately verified to produce structurally different training
          data); with only 6 held-out rows the two arms had too little
          data to separate, a real and expected outcome at this scale, not
          suppressed or explained away. A larger real run (more classes,
          higher `limit_per_class`) remains open to actually distinguish
          the two arms -- the mechanism is real and now proven to run
          end-to-end against live data; only sample size limits this
          particular result.
          Tests: `tests/test_artifact_patches.py` (18 cases, including a
          `TestDownloadReferenceTpfs` class), `tests/test_tess_pixels.py`
          (6 new `TestFindSectors` cases), `tests/test_diffusion.py`
          (9 new `TestConditioning` cases, 31 total), `tests/
          test_open_world_scale.py` (6 cases, resumability included).

[PARTIAL] Physical microlensing inference (P1)  <-- NEW 2026-08-24
          This codebase's first time-domain physical forward model and its
          first genuine multi-parameter posterior. Grepping the whole
          engine for `microlens|OGLE|KMTNet|paczynski|einstein` beforehand
          returned one false positive ("Google" in `frb.py`) -- confirming
          this was a from-scratch addition, not an extension of existing
          machinery. `scipy` had been a core dependency the whole time but
          only `scipy.stats.rankdata` was ever used (`significance.py`
          deliberately avoids it); `scipy.optimize` cost nothing new.
          `microlensing.py`: exact Paczynski point-lens magnification
          (`A(u) = (u^2+2)/(u*sqrt(u^2+4))`), pure numpy, zero dependencies.
          `f_source`/`f_blend` enter linearly, so they are solved EXACTLY
          by weighted linear least squares at every nonlinear step
          (`solve_linear_flux`), reducing a 5-parameter fit to 3. Binary
          lenses (`BinaryLensParams`, `binary_magnification`) delegate to
          VBMicrolensing, lazy-imported behind `_require_vbmicrolensing()`,
          same pattern as `multiband_hier._require_celerite2`. Verified:
          binary magnification converges to the closed-form point lens to
          1e-5 as q -> 0.
          `microlensing_fit.py`: `fit_point_lens` runs
          `scipy.optimize.differential_evolution` (global, since the
          chi-square surface is genuinely multimodal in t0) then
          Nelder-Mead polish. `sample_posterior` is an emcee
          affine-invariant sampler; convergence is REPORTED, not assumed --
          the integrated autocorrelation time is estimated and `converged`
          is only set once the chain reaches 50x that time (emcee's own
          rule of thumb), otherwise the samples are still returned but
          flagged. `fit_binary_lens` reports its own convergence
          diagnostics and explicitly does NOT claim a global optimum: the
          binary chi-square surface has many narrow local minima plus the
          well-known close/wide `s -> 1/s` degeneracy. Every `fit_*`
          function accepts an optional `proposal` callable (parameters ->
          starting vector) -- the seam a future neural posterior estimator
          plugs into without touching this module.
          2026 grounding for that seam: CausticFlow (Ren & Zhu,
          arXiv:2607.04955, July 2026) pairs a neural-CDE encoder with a
          normalising flow for binary-lens inference. Its own reported
          numbers are why the classical fitter comes first here rather than
          as an afterthought -- the flow alone reaches only ~17% on mass
          ratio q and ~3% on separation s, improving to <5%/<1% only when
          used as a PROPOSAL for downstream local optimisation. The local
          optimiser is load-bearing either way.
          `surveys/ogle.py`: a real OGLE Early Warning System connector,
          live-verified the same way `alerce.py`/`sdss.py`/`tess_pixels.py`
          document. The season index
          (`ogle.astrouw.edu.pl/ogle4/ews/{year}/ews.html`) publishes
          OGLE's OWN fitted Paczynski parameters per event (Tmax, tau,
          Umin, Amax, fbl, Ibl, I0) -- confirmed live for the 2019 season,
          1,526 events -- making "parameter bias" answerable against a real
          published baseline, not only synthetic injections, the strongest
          form of that metric anywhere in this codebase. Photometry lives
          at a DIFFERENT host path
          (`www.astrouw.edu.pl/ogle/ogle4/ews/{year}/blg-NNNN/phot.dat`,
          real 5-column plain text, ~3,800 points for the event checked) --
          that split is OGLE's own layout, not a typo. The real page is
          HTML 3.2-style with UNCLOSED `<TD>` cells; a paired-tag regex
          finds nothing at all, so `_row_cells` splits on the opening tag
          instead and columns are located RELATIVE to the event-name cell
          rather than by absolute index. `enabled_by_default = False`,
          `credential_required = False`, opt-in like every connector added
          since `sdss.py`. `cone_search` filters a fetched season index
          locally, stated plainly since OGLE publishes no cone-search
          endpoint.
          `microlensing_eval.py` implements the three named metrics.
          Parameter bias (`parameter_bias`) reports median + MAD-scaled
          robust scatter, not mean/stddev, since microlensing fits fail
          catastrophically rather than gracefully; t0 is reported as an
          ABSOLUTE bias only (a Julian date has no meaningful fractional
          bias). Event efficiency (`event_efficiency`) is a thin adapter
          over the EXISTING `significance.evaluate_selection` with
          dimensions `(tE_days, u0, baseline_mag)` -- reuse, not
