          reimplementation, Wilson 95% intervals and Kish effective sample
          size come for free. Posterior coverage (`posterior_coverage`,
          `sbc_ranks`) follows `frb.credible_region_containment`'s
          simulate-refit-count pattern, generalised from a HEALPix sky
          region to parametric credible intervals, plus SBC rank
          statistics (Talts et al.) whose histogram must be uniform for a
          calibrated posterior. Notably this is the first time ANY credible
          interval in this codebase has been coverage-tested at all --
          `multiband_hier.credible_interval` still never has been.
          Real-data verification, the point of the whole item: fitting
          3,335 real OGLE photometry points for event 2019-BLG-0001 against
          OGLE's own published parameters recovered t0 within ~7 minutes,
          tE within 0.05%, and u0 within 0.3% (chi2/dof = 3.39, typical for
          crowded-bulge photometry). `run_validation_study` was then run on
          that same event's REAL cadence and REAL error bars (not an
          invented uniform cadence), 12 trials, 41.4s: 12/12 events
          detected above the delta-chi2=25 threshold; tE median fractional
          bias 2.2e-05, u0 -0.13%; coverage slightly over-conservative at
          this small trial count (nominal 0.5 -> empirical 0.67, 0.68 ->
          0.83, 0.9 -> 1.00); SBC normalised mean ranks t0=0.52, tE=0.40,
          u0=0.35 (0.5 is exactly calibrated). The coverage metric itself
          was separately validated against deliberately mis-calibrated
          posteriors before trusting it on real data: an artificially
          narrow interval showed empirical coverage 0.41 against a nominal
          0.9, an artificially wide one showed 1.00, and a genuinely
          calibrated construction showed 0.91 -- confirming the metric
          detects both directions of miscalibration rather than only
          reporting a number that happens to look plausible.
          Honest limits, stated rather than hidden: Gaia contributes no
          epoch photometry to this until DR4 (expected 2026-12-02, already
          tracked elsewhere in this file); KMTNet is deferred entirely --
          its public event pages carry no downloadable photometry and its
          data remain proprietary until one year after the observing
          season, so there is nothing to build a connector against yet;
          binary-lens fitting reports convergence diagnostics rather than
          claiming global optimality, by design, for the reason CausticFlow
          documents. Not wired into `rpc.py`, `scoring.WEIGHTS`, or
          `evidence.py` -- an opt-in research module like every other one
          added this session.
          Tests: `tests/test_microlensing.py` (18 cases), `tests/
          test_microlensing_fit.py` (15 cases), `tests/test_surveys_ogle.py`
          (22 cases), `tests/test_microlensing_eval.py` (24 cases) --
          79 total, all passing.

[PARTIAL] The method comparison needs re-running at scale  <-- IMPORTANT
          The first injection-recovery study ran on only 54 ZTF sequences
          with 8 injected anomalies. On that data the simplest baseline
          (PCA reconstruction, ROC-AUC 0.742) BEAT both deep models
          (autoencoder 0.622, VAE 0.609). The VAE early-stopped after 7
          epochs on 43 training rows.
          That is a real result and exactly what plan section 13 predicts
          can happen, but at n=54 it mostly says the deep models are
          data-starved. It is NOT yet evidence that deep learning does
          not help here.
          => A resumable `stageb.compare` job now checkpoints each independent
             seed, records a dataset fingerprint, survey/release/band strata,
             sidecar schemas, preprocessing contract, and empirical confidence
             intervals. It is exposed in Experiments but never starts
             automatically. It still must be run on a Stage-B population
             (thousands of curves) before drawing a paper conclusion.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 6 — CROSS-SURVEY ENGINE
---------------------------------------------------------------------

[KNOWN]   TESS cannot resolve individual stars  <-- MEASURED, IMPORTANT
          TESS pixels are 21 arcsec. On the real data, five different ZTF
          sources all matched the SAME TESS object (TIC 159717514),
          because the TESS aperture is a sum over the whole
          neighbourhood. Counting that as independent confirmation would
          manufacture cross-survey agreement for every star sitting near
          a bright variable.
          Now handled: such matches are marked `blended` and excluded
          from `resolved_surveys`. On the current dataset this takes the
          count of genuinely two-survey-resolved objects from 5 to 0.
          => Resolving a specific star in TESS requires PSF fitting or
             deblending on target-pixel files. Phase 2 now supports explicit
             candidate-scale TPF downloads and aperture extraction, but not
             PSF fitting; it therefore remains neighbourhood photometry, not
             stellar photometry, and does not restore resolved-survey credit.
           UPDATE 2026-08-19: TGLC (TESS-Gaia Light Curve, MAST HLSP) supplies
          exactly this as a finished product: it forward-models each FFI
          with an effective PSF using Gaia DR3 positions/magnitudes as
           priors, reaching <=2% photometric precision at T=16 even in
           crowded fields — without downloading a single TPF. TGLC is now
           wired through the connector with a robust MAD-of-differences
           per-cadence error estimate when its product omits FLUX_ERR; the
           provenance marks that estimate explicitly. It still does not
           restore resolved-survey credit until target-specific recovery is
           validated against blends and injection tests.
          QLP (real bulk TESS coverage, no PSF deblending) IS wired in and
          was used to take tess_only/gaia_tess from refused to scored —
          real numbers in that same Phase 8 entry, including a measured
          pipeline-mixing effect from combining it with the 3 pre-existing
          SPOC curves. It does not resolve the blending problem THIS entry
          describes; QLP's photometry is still 21-arcsec neighbourhood
          photometry same as SPOC, just for vastly more targets.


[PARTIAL] Grouping anchors on the largest catalogue
          group_sources() uses whichever survey has the most detections
          as the anchor. That is a defensible default but it means the
          object list is defined by one survey's selection function —
          a selection bias worth documenting per section 36. The crossmatch
          response now includes anchor share and per-survey match rates, so
          downstream studies can stratify or rerun explicitly. UPDATE
          2026-08-21: callers can now pass an explicit `anchor_survey` through
          `crossmatch.run`/`crossmatch.profile` (and the UI). The response
          records `anchor_policy` and the requested anchor. The default still
          uses the largest catalogue, with lexical tie-breaking so mapping
          insertion order cannot change a result. This controls the population
          denominator; it does not remove the underlying selection bias.

[PARTIAL] Cross-survey zero-point and color-term calibration  <-- NEW 2026-08-22
          `photometric_calibration.py` fits
          `comparison_mag - anchor_mag = zero_point + color_term * color`
          per stratum (survey pair, weighted by combined uncertainty) against
          matched pairs from `crossmatch.group_sources` -- reusing that
          matching machinery unchanged rather than reimplementing it. Follows
          `significance.py`'s exact shape: additive-only diagnostic,
          `SCHEMA_VERSION`-tagged, its own `save()` under
          `results/photometric_calibration/`, never mutating the matched
          photometry and never touching `evidence.WEIGHTS`/
          `scoring.combine()` -- adopting a fitted zero-point into scoring
          later would have to bump `evidence.WEIGHT_VERSION` explicitly, the
          same restraint already documented for GW/FRB/artifact-weight
          calibration above. The weighted-ridge solver mirrors
          `significance.fit_selection_model`'s ridge solver shape (same
          deterministic closed-form fit, no sigmoid link since this is
          linear, not classification).
          Closed a real gap to make this possible at all: neither
          `surveys/gaia.py` nor `surveys/panstarrs.py` exposed photometric
          *uncertainties` before this -- `gaia.py` now requests the
          `phot_{g,bp,rp}_mean_flux(_error)` columns and derives a magnitude
          error via the standard flux-ratio formula
          (`gaia.magnitude_error_from_flux`/`photometric_errors`, since
          gaia_source has no direct `phot_*_mag_error` column); `panstarrs.py`
          now also requests the `*MeanPSFMagErr` fields DR2's mean table
          already publishes but this connector never asked for.
          Still explicitly open, not glossed over: `surveys/sdss.py` remains
          catalogue-only (no photometry fields at all), so SDSS pairs are
          silently unavailable to this calibration until that connector is
          extended -- `uncertainty_coverage`/`n_pairs` in the returned report
          make a two-survey result visible as such rather than presenting it
          as if SDSS had been included. Per-night/camera stratification is
          supported (`stratum_by_object_id`) but this module does not itself
          derive a night/camera label from any survey's metadata -- the
          caller must supply one; without it every pair falls into one
          `"default"` stratum and the fit degrades gracefully to a single
          global zero-point/color-term.
          Tests: `tests/test_photometric_calibration.py` -- synthetic matched
          pairs with a known injected zero-point/color-term (recoverable by
          construction, same discipline `artifact.calibrate_from_injection`
          uses), plus direct tests of the new Gaia flux-to-magnitude-error
          conversion. No network.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 7 — EXPLAINABILITY & CANDIDATE RANKING
---------------------------------------------------------------------

[DONE]    Per-candidate feature attribution  <-- NEW 2026-08-28
          Real SHAP was deliberately NOT used, decided this session rather
          than defaulted into: the 4 live detectors (`anomaly.py`) are
          heterogeneous -- `run_isolation_forest` is tree-based (SHAP
          `TreeExplainer`-compatible) but `run_lof`/`run_one_class_svm`/
          `run_pca_reconstruction` are not, and `LocalOutlierFactor` isn't
          even fit in novelty mode, so there is no `decision_function` for
          a modified row without a full refit. Using the real `shap`
          package would have meant mixing `TreeExplainer` and
          `KernelExplainer` per detector (two different theoretical
          footings for one ensemble's explanation), a new dependency with
          installer/packaging impact, and real per-candidate latency from
          `KernelExplainer`'s sampling. A future session should read this
          entry before re-litigating that choice.
          Built instead: `attribution.py` (`explain_candidate`), a model-
          agnostic occlusion method that reuses `anomaly.detect()`
          completely unchanged. For one candidate row, each raw feature in
          turn is replaced with the population median for that feature
          (excluding the row's own value), the full ensemble is rerun
          (same contamination/seed), and `impact = baseline_consensus -
          occluded_consensus` is recorded -- a positive impact means that
          feature's actual value is what is raising the score above what a
          typical value would produce. This reruns the ensemble once per
          feature (28 today), so it is a real, multi-second, explicit
          per-candidate action -- never computed automatically for a whole
          batch, matching the cost class `ExperimentsView`'s existing "Run
          ablation" button already established for this kind of research
          action.
          A candidate's row is re-located after each rerun by its
          identity's `path` (unique per object, already the pattern
          `featurematrix.join_gaia_columns` uses), not by positional index,
          since `anomaly.prepare()`'s finite-mask can drop different rows
          across reruns in principle even though it never does here in
          practice (occlusion always writes a finite median).
          Wired live: `rpc.py` (`candidates.explain`, looks up the matrix
          row via the candidate's own `path` field -- `Candidate.path` is
          already copied verbatim from the same `FeatureMatrix` identity
          `path` at candidate-build time, so no id-recomputation was
          needed), `src-tauri/src/lib.rs` (`engine_candidate_explain`),
          `engine.ts` (`AttributionResult`, `candidateExplain`), and
          `ExplainPanel.tsx` (new `FeatureAttribution` component beside the
          existing `ScoreDrivers` -- a different, deeper question: Score-
          Drivers explains the composite score's already-transparent
          weights, this explains the raw, opaque, unsupervised ensemble
          score's dependence on the underlying features).
          Tests: `tests/test_attribution.py` (6 cases: a constructed
          single-feature outlier gets that feature ranked top by impact, a
          non-dominant row's impacts stay small, a non-finite feature
          returns `explainable: False` rather than raising, components are
          sorted by |impact|, `top` truncates the list, the identifying
          `path` round-trips) and two `tests/test_rpc.py` cases (found and
          not-found candidate ids through `candidates.explain`). `cargo
          check` and `tsc --noEmit` both clean; full Vitest suite green
          (64 passed).

[DONE]    Confidence + physical labels on feature attribution  <-- NEW 2026-08-31
          Literature motivation, read from the primary source this session:
          Ortiz Manrique & Boquien, "Interpreting anomaly detection of SDSS
          spectra," A&A 703, A242 (2025) (arXiv:2510.05235), which argues
          interpretable anomaly detection -- not raw detection scores -- is
          what makes large spectroscopic anomaly searches "scalable,
          transparent, and physically meaningful" for deciding what gets
          followed up. `attribution.py`'s existing `explain_candidate`
          reported a single occlusion impact per feature from ONE reference
          point (the population median) with no confidence signal and a raw
          column name -- exactly the two gaps this literature's argument
          points at.
          Two gaps closed, both additive; `explain_candidate` itself is
          UNCHANGED (its 6 existing tests pass verbatim):
          (1) `engine/astra/feature_glossary.py` (new) -- a pure lookup
          table mapping every raw feature name (`features.FEATURE_NAMES`,
          `featurematrix.GAIA_JOIN_COLUMNS`, `.STELLAR_MANIFOLD_COLUMNS`) to
          a human label, physical unit (where the underlying feature
          function documents one -- e.g. `time_span_days` -> "days",
          `gaia_parallax` -> "mas"), and description -- restating what
          `features.py`/`featurematrix.py` already document, not a new
          citation. Falls back to a title-cased raw name for any future
          feature this glossary hasn't been updated for, so coverage gaps
          degrade gracefully rather than breaking attribution.
          (2) `attribution.explain_candidate_stable` (new function,
          `attribution.py`) -- for only the `stability_top` (default 5)
          highest-|impact| features from an unchanged first median-only
          pass, additionally occludes at the 25th/75th percentile of the
          "other" population (reusing the already-computed median result,
          not a third rerun) and reports `impact_mean/std/min/max` plus a
          boolean `stable` (sign-consistent across all three references,
          spread below half the mean magnitude). A full bootstrap over
          every raw feature (not just the top few) was rejected for cost --
          it would multiply the already-expensive full-ensemble-rerun-per-
          feature cost across all ~28-43 columns instead of ~5. Every
          component also carries `feature_glossary.describe(...)`'s label/
          unit/description, and a deterministic, template-built (no LLM
          call) `narrative` string names the top 2-3 STABLE drivers in
          physical terms; when the single strongest raw impact is itself
          unstable, the narrative says so explicitly ("reference-sensitive
          ... treat with caution") rather than presenting a shaky number as
          confident.
          The SHAP-rejection reasoning in the entry above is UNCHANGED by
          this addition -- still occlusion, still no new dependency, still
          one uniform method across all 4 detectors; not re-litigated here.
          Deliberately NOT done, and why: persisting/caching attribution on
          candidate records, and linking the attribution narrative into
          `scoring.py`'s composite score -- both would start pulling this
          "diagnostic evidence only" module toward ranking, which this
          codebase's convention explicitly forbids. Grepped `scoring.py`
          and `evidence.py` after this pass: zero hits for `attribution`,
          `attribution_eval`, or `feature_glossary` in either, confirming
          neither was folded into ranking.
          Wired live: `rpc.py` (`_handle_candidate_explain` gains an
          optional `stable` param on the existing `candidates.explain`
          dispatch key -- no new key, and the default/no-`stable` response
          stays byte-identical to before this pass), `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`
          (`engine_candidate_explain` gains an optional `stable` arg),
          `engine.ts` (`AttributionResult` widened with optional
          `narrative`/`label`/`unit`/`description`/`impact_mean`/
          `impact_std`/`impact_min`/`impact_max`/`stable` fields, all
          optional so the plain-`explain_candidate` response shape still
          type-checks), and `ExplainPanel.tsx`'s `FeatureAttribution` (new
          opt-in "Include confidence" checkbox beside the existing button --
          the cheap default path is unchanged and this cost is disclosed
          before a user pays it, matching this panel's existing busy-
          gating discipline).
          New `engine/astra/attribution_eval.py` (offline self-check,
          confirmed absent from `rpc.py` by its own
          `test_not_referenced_by_rpc`, same convention every other eval
          module in this codebase follows): `evaluate_stability_quality`
          demonstrates `stable=True` on a constructed symmetric/unimodal
          reference population and `stable=False` on a constructed 70/30
          bimodal one (the candidate matches the minority cluster, so the
          majority-driven median/25th-percentile reference and the
          candidate's-own-cluster 75th-percentile reference imply opposite
          conclusions about how anomalous the candidate is -- the occlusion
          impact flips sign across quantiles, the concrete, measured
          version of "when is a single-median reference NOT trustworthy").
          `evaluate_glossary_coverage` asserts every shipped feature column
          has a real (non-fallback) glossary entry.
          Tests: `tests/test_attribution.py` gained 7 `TestExplainCandidateStable`
          cases (dominant feature keeps top rank; stable=True on a
          symmetric population; stable=False on the constructed skewed
          population; non-empty narrative naming the dominant feature's
          label; narrative explicitly flags an unstable top component;
          `explain_candidate`'s own output shape/behaviour is unchanged;
          non-finite feature still returns `explainable: False`). New
          `tests/test_feature_glossary.py` (7 cases: known-feature lookup,
          unmapped-name fallback, full coverage of every shipped feature
          name, unit-aware/unitless/NaN value formatting). New
          `tests/test_attribution_eval.py` (3 cases: both eval functions'
          expected results on the constructed populations, plus
          `test_not_referenced_by_rpc`). `tests/test_rpc.py` gained one
          case exercising `stable: True` through `_handle_candidate_explain`
          end to end. Full offline suite, `cargo check`, and `tsc --noEmit`
          all re-verified clean after this pass.

[DONE]    Local candidate feed file (not a network broadcast)  <-- NEW 2026-08-28
          Scope decision, made explicitly rather than defaulted into: ASTRA
          is a local desktop app (Tauri) with no user accounts, hosting, or
          existing outbound network infrastructure -- confirmed by grep
          across `engine/` and `src-tauri/src/` for watchdog/`notify`/
          `axum`/`warp`/`hyper`/static-file-serving: zero hits, so a real
          network push would be entirely new infrastructure, not an
          extension of anything. Picking and authenticating against a real
          third-party publish target (a hosted API, a webhook service) was
          judged a much bigger commitment than "let a researcher share
          results outside the app" requires. Built instead: `broadcast.py`
          (`generate_feed`), which writes a LOCAL JSON file a researcher
          can point external tools at or send manually -- UI copy says
          "local feed file", never "publish"/"broadcast to the internet",
          so it never implies network behaviour that does not exist.
          Reuses two existing precedents rather than inventing new ones:
          the `>= threshold` cutoff mirrors `review.py`'s own fixed,
          out-of-band `scores >= 0.5` precedent (`review.py:125`); the
          envelope shape (`schema_version` + flat candidate array) mirrors
          `alerts.py`'s inbound packet envelope, the one other alert-shaped
          format already in this codebase. Unlike `exports.py`'s
          timestamped snapshots, the feed writes to a STABLE path
          (`{name}_feed.json`) that overwrites on each call -- a feed is
          one resource that updates in place, not a growing pile of
          snapshots.
          Wired live: `rpc.py` (`candidates.broadcast`), `src-tauri/src/
          lib.rs` (`engine_candidate_broadcast`), `engine.ts`
          (`BroadcastFeedResult`, `broadcastFeed`), and `ReportsView.tsx`'s
          existing "Export candidate run" panel gained a "Generate local
          feed file" section (threshold input + button + copyable path),
          extending rather than replacing the existing CSV/FITS/PDF export
          UI pattern.
          No changes to `exports.py`, `review.py`, `alerts.py`, or
          candidate scoring. Tests: `tests/test_broadcast.py` (4 cases:
          threshold filtering, envelope schema fields, stable-path
          overwrite on a second call, empty-feed handling) and one
          `tests/test_rpc.py` case. `cargo check` and `tsc --noEmit` both
          clean; full Vitest suite green (64 passed).

[DONE]    Multi-reviewer citizen-science voting  <-- NEW 2026-08-28
          `labels` (`metadata.py:40-43`) is one row per candidate, last
          write wins -- exactly right for a single expert reviewer, and
          already trusted unchanged by `active_review.py`, `review.
          evaluate`, and the ranker. Citizen-science voting needs the
          opposite shape, so it was added as a genuinely separate,
          additive table rather than touching `labels`: `label_votes`
          (`metadata.py`, SCHEMA_VERSION 7->8) is append-only, no `ON
          CONFLICT` -- multiple independent reviewers, or the same
          reviewer more than once, each add a new row. Modelled on
          `followup_requests`' (see the C14-equivalent entry above) same
          one-candidate-to-many-rows shape, not reinvented.
          Stated limitation, not worked around: `reviewer_id` is free text
          with no account/auth system behind it (this app has none) -- it
          cannot guarantee one-vote-per-person, the same class of caveat
          `active_review.py`'s reviewer-agreement metric already carries
          for true inter-rater identity.
          Promotion from votes to the single authoritative label
          (`candidates.promote_vote_consensus`) is ALWAYS an explicit,
          gated action -- never triggered automatically by casting a vote.
          The gate (`MIN_VOTES = 3`, `MIN_AGREEMENT = 0.6`, both fixed and
          overridable, in `review.py`'s own stated "deliberately fixed
          rather than selected on this same set" style) mirrors `review.
          evaluate`'s exact "build a gate dict reporting thresholds AND
          observed counts, short-circuit with a reason before computing
          anything" discipline (`review.py`'s `MIN_LABELS`/
          `MIN_PER_CLASS`). When the gate passes, promotion calls the
          EXISTING `record_label` unchanged -- never forked -- so every
          downstream consumer of `labels` sees a normal label, with no
          knowledge that it originated from a vote.
          Wired live: `rpc.py` (`candidates.vote`, `candidates.votes`,
          `candidates.vote_promote`), `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`
          (`engine_candidate_vote`/`_votes`/`_vote_promote`), `engine.ts`
          (`LabelVote`, `VoteTally`, `VotePromotion`), and
          `CandidateNotesPanel.tsx` gained a "Community votes" section
          below the existing single-reviewer label buttons: a reviewer-ID
          field, per-label vote buttons, a live tally (majority label
          highlighted), and a "Promote consensus" button whose result
          message reports the gate outcome either way.
          No changes to `labels`' schema, `put_label`, `review.py`,
          `active_review.py`, or the ranker. Tests: `tests/test_
          candidates.py::TestLabelVoting` (10 cases: cast/read round trip,
          multiple reviewers accumulate rather than overwrite, unknown
          label rejected, empty reviewer_id rejected, tally majority/
          agreement correctness, tally-with-no-votes reports None not
          0, promotion below vote count / below agreement both report the
          gate reason without touching `labels`, promotion at threshold
          updates the authoritative label, a second promotion call is
          safe) and one `tests/test_rpc.py` round-trip case. `cargo check`
          and `tsc --noEmit` both clean; full Vitest suite green
          (64 passed).

[PARTIAL] Catalogue cross-reference (SIMBAD / VSX / TNS)
          An explicit, non-blocking enrichment action now queries SIMBAD and
          VSX through VizieR, plus TNS when protected credentials are
          configured. Results use a versioned 30-day local cache and report
          match/no-match/offline/rate-limit/unavailable states instead of
          silently turning missing network evidence into novelty. Cached
          results can be used offline and update catalog-novelty scoring.
          TNS live behaviour still depends on a user-provided API key and all
          provider contracts need continued monitoring as their services evolve.


[KNOWN]   Composite score renormalises over available components
          With physical_inconsistency and catalog_novelty both missing,
          the real run used only 0.75 of the total weight. Scores are
          renormalised so candidates are not punished for unqueried
          evidence, but that means a score of 0.70 computed from 75% of
          the evidence is NOT comparable with 0.70 from 100% of it.
          `weight_used` is reported alongside every score for this
          reason. Cross-run comparison must check it. This part of the
          gap is inherent and honestly reported, not a bug -- it is not
          addressed by the update below.
          UPDATE 2026-08-28: the separate, previously real gap -- raw
          consensus (`EnsembleResult.consensus`) was only ever min-max
          rescaled per detector and calibrated (`calibrate_scores`,
          `anomaly.py`) against its own batch, so a 0.70 in one `detect`
          call meant something different from a 0.70 in another -- is now
          closed for the live scoring path. `_handle_detect` (`rpc.py`)
          loads a persisted, per-feature-matrix-name calibration reference
          (`anomaly.load_calibration_reference`) before scoring, passes it
          into `EnsembleResult.ranked()`/`.to_dict()` (both now accept an
          optional `reference`), then folds this run's consensus scores
          into that reference for the next run
          (`anomaly.update_calibration_reference`, FIFO-capped at 20,000
          scores per name). The first-ever run for a name still falls back
          to batch-relative calibration (empty reference) by construction;
          from the second run onward, `consensus_calibrated` is comparable
          across runs of the same matrix name. `calibration_report`'s
          `reference_external`/`reference_rows` fields make which mode
          applied explicit in every response, same discipline as
          `weight_used` above.

[PARTIAL] Period-luminosity class centres are approximate
          PERIOD_LUMINOSITY_CLASSES holds rough class centres for Delta
          Scuti, RR Lyrae and Cepheids. It is a screen for gross
          inconsistency, not a classifier, and has no extinction
          correction — reddening will shift real objects off the locus
          and can manufacture apparent inconsistency in the plane.
          Gaia-derived A_G/E(BP-RP) values are now consumed when available;
          the uncorrected screen remains the explicit fallback.


[PARTIAL] Artifact indicator weights are hand-set  <-- UPDATED 2026-08-18
          The weights in artifact.assess are judgement calls combined as
          independent probabilities (now centralised in artifact.WEIGHTS,
          previously inline literals -- same values, just a single place to
          read and change them).
          INVESTIGATED: no external dataset fits. SNAD's ZTF DR3 "Dataset of
          artefacts" (arXiv:2504.08053, 1,127 objects / 1,213 labels), the
          candidate identified for this in an earlier pass, turns out to
          publish 28x28 / 63x63 FITS IMAGE cutouts with no ZTF object IDs
          and no light-curve data at all. artifact.assess operates entirely
          on one curve's own light-curve statistics (period, chi-square,
          amplitude/error ratio, kurtosis, change-point score), none of
          which an image cutout can supply. SNAD's broader anomaly tooling
          (zwad) does publish real light-curve FEATURES with real object
          IDs, but no bundled artifact/real ground-truth labels to
          calibrate against. ASTRA's own human-in-the-loop labels (1 total,
          "interesting", zero "artifact") are nowhere near usable either.
          BUILT INSTEAD: artifact.calibrate_from_injection(), a synthetic
          injection-recovery study for the six indicators that threshold a
          statistic of one curve's own features (sampling_period,
          low_significance, consistent_with_constant, sparse_sampling,
          step_change, extreme_outliers -- collected in
          FEATURE_INDICATOR_NAMES). Real, unremarkable synthetic variables
          get label 0; each "artifact" curve carries exactly one
          deliberately injected defect, so the ground truth is true by
          construction, the same idea evaluate.py already uses for the
          anomaly-detection ensemble. Every synthetic curve goes through
          the real features.extract()/artifact.assess() code paths -- the
          study does not reimplement assess()'s threshold logic, so it
          cannot silently drift from what assess() actually checks.
          The remaining four WEIGHTS entries (single_instrument,
          blended_photometry, agreement_not_independent,
          period_disagreement) are structural/categorical -- cross-survey
          resolution and period agreement, not a threshold on one curve's
          own statistics -- and a single synthetic curve cannot represent
          them; they are not calibrated by this study and stay hand-set.
          MEASURED (5 seeds, 150 real + 150 artifact per seed, held out
          30%): every one of the six fires reliably on its own injected
          defect (100% across a 60-sample verification sweep per kind) and
          essentially never on a clean synthetic real curve (0/80 for five
          of six; sampling_period false-fired 2/80, ~2.5%, consistent with
          this codebase's own documented period-aliasing behaviour).
          Calibrated weights came back 0.95-1.0 for all six -- versus the
          hand-set 0.20-0.35 -- with held-out AUC improving 0.9807 ->
          0.9939.
          NOT ADOPTED, by explicit choice, and correctly so: a synthetic
          "real" class this clean and well-separated will always make an
          indicator look more reliable than it is on messy real data (a
          genuinely eruptive variable with real large outliers, a real
          eclipsing binary with a real step, a real short-baseline
          detection). A weight of exactly 1.0 is also a real behavioural
          change, not just a bigger number: it pins the pre-clearing
          likelihood at 100% the instant that indicator fires alone,
          removing any influence from what else did or did not fire. The
          held-out AUC comparison is weak evidence either way -- both
          numbers are already near the ceiling because the synthetic
          populations are easy to separate. WEIGHTS keeps its hand-set
          values; this is a validated MECHANISM (the code runs, produces
          directionally sensible, reproducible results) rather than a
          calibration ready to deploy.
          => Worth re-running once either a harder/adversarial synthetic
             "real" class exists (deliberately include sparse, noisy, or
             large-outlier REAL variables to measure genuine false-positive
             rates) or ASTRA's own human-in-the-loop labels reach a usable
             "artifact" count.
          UPDATE 2026-08-19: the first half of that unblock condition is now
          done. `_synthetic_real(..., hard=True)` draws awkward but genuine
          objects -- sparsely sampled (12-34 points over 300-900 days), near
          the noise floor (amplitude 1.2-2.5x the error), or carrying 2-5
          real large excursions -- and `DEFAULT_HARD_REAL_FRACTION = 0.4` of
          the real class is drawn that way. The raw train-split precision was
          replaced by `smoothed_precision()`: Laplace smoothing (alpha 2.0)
          so a 3-of-3 firing no longer reads as a perfect indicator, and a
          hard ceiling `MAX_CALIBRATED_WEIGHT = 0.85` so no weight can
          saturate the noisy-OR. `calibrate_recorded()` plus the new
          `artifact.calibrate` RPC give the study a provenance record; it was
          previously the only study in the codebase without one.
          RE-MEASURED against the hardened population (5 seeds, 150+150 per
          seed, held out 30%): held-out AUC 0.9689 -> 0.9815, and the
          proposed weights are
              sampling_period          0.35 -> 0.85 (ceiling)
              low_significance         0.30 -> 0.85 (ceiling)
              consistent_with_constant 0.25 -> 0.85 (ceiling)
              sparse_sampling          0.20 -> 0.5490
              step_change              0.20 -> 0.85 (ceiling)
              extreme_outliers         0.25 -> 0.85 (ceiling)
          STILL NOT ADOPTED, and the new numbers say why more clearly than
          the old ones did. `sparse_sampling` fell to 0.549 precisely because
          the hardened population contains real sparse variables it fires on
          -- that is a genuine false-positive rate being measured for the
          first time, and it is the one number here that the data actually
          set. The other five sit exactly at the 0.85 ceiling, which means
          the ceiling and not the evidence is choosing them: even the
          hardened synthetic "real" class still cannot contradict those five.
          Adopting would also move a single-indicator firing from below the
          0.3 band to at or above the 0.6 "probably an artifact" band, so the
          verdict thresholds at artifact.py:88-91 would have to be
          re-derived, not carried over.
          => Adopt only after the OTHER half of the unblock condition is met
             (real human "artifact" labels), or after the hard-real
             population is extended until the five ceiling-pinned indicators
             come off the ceiling on their own. The mechanism is now sound
             and recorded; the population is still too kind.

[PARTIAL] Candidate investigation UI (plan section 18)  <-- UPDATED 2026-08-19
          The desktop shell now exposes Dashboard, Projects, Acquire, Sky,
          Curves, Candidates, Cross-survey, Experiments, Models, Reports,
          and Settings through a typed internal navigation model. Projects
          can be created, opened, archived/restored, and validated; project
          candidate outputs, labels, catalog enrichment, and exports are
          scoped to the selected workspace.
          Candidate detail now renders the explanation drivers, recommended
          actions, artifact verdict/likelihood, evidence counts, research
          notes, and a bounded combined BJD_TDB timeline with explicit TESS
          blend warnings.
          CORRECTION: this entry previously said per-survey curve overlays
          and project-context threading were still absent. Both are done.
          CandidateWorkspace.tsx:441-449 already renders a per-survey
          LightCurvePlot for up to 6 curves inside the combined timeline
          (labelled resolved/blended), and every routed view in
          App.tsx:112-130 receives and consumes `projectId` except
          SettingsView, which correctly does not need it.
          FIXED 2026-08-19: the main candidate curve now has an interactive
          period-fold control, reusing the existing `engine.curveFold` RPC
          and the same fold/unfold UI pattern already used in
          `CurveExplorer.tsx`. The period field pre-fills from the
          candidate's own `features.best_period_days` when present and
          positive, and is otherwise free-text. Timeline overlay curves
          (CandidateWorkspace.tsx timeline block) remain unfolded by design —
          they exist to compare raw multi-survey coverage, where folding
          each independently would need a per-curve period control and
          adds UI complexity for a secondary view; the primary curve is
          where folding matters and is now covered.
          FIXED 2026-08-19 (2): the dedicated explanation pane now exists as
          its own sidebar section ("Explain", `src/components/ExplainPanel.tsx`),
          built on the modern ui.tsx primitives rather than extending the
          older CandidateWorkspace conventions.
          The substance of this was a DISPLAY gap, not a capability gap:
          every number it renders was already computed and already shipped
          inside each candidate record, and none of it reached the screen.
          Specifically, `ArtifactAssessment.to_dict()` has always emitted
          `indicators[]` (name/weight/detail) and `clearing_evidence[]`, and
          the UI showed only `likelihood` and `verdict` -- the assessment's
          entire argument was discarded and only its conclusion displayed.
          `ScoreBreakdown.to_dict()` has always emitted per-component
          `weighted` contributions and `reasons`; the TS type declared only
          `components`, so the frontend could not have used them.
          Two things the pane deliberately shows rather than smooths over:
          a component's score sits next to its weighted contribution, because
          0.9 at weight 0.05 and 0.5 at weight 0.25 rank the opposite way
          from how the raw scores read; and `weight_used` is always visible
          with an explicit note when it is below 1.0, because the total
          renormalises over whatever evidence existed.
          One small engine addition supported it: `CrossSurveyProfile.to_dict()`
          now also emits `weights` and `weighted` alongside `components`
          (evidence.py). Additive only -- WEIGHT_VERSION stays 2, no stored
          profile changes value.
