[KNOWN]   Feature ablation shows no family is clearly load-bearing
          Dropping any one summary-feature family changed AUC by at most
          +/-0.018 from the 0.824 baseline, and three of five REMOVALS
          scored higher than keeping everything. That points to redundant
          or noisy columns rather than to a family worth removing; the
          differences are within what one injection seed can produce.
          => Needs repeated seeds and confidence intervals before any
             feature is cut.


[KNOWN]   Code version is a source hash, not a commit
          The project is not a git repository, so experiment provenance
          hashes every .py file under engine/astra instead. Arguably
          stronger than a commit — it changes if and only if the code
          that ran changed, uncommitted edits included — but it cannot
          be used to check out the exact source again.
          => `git init` would make provenance recoverable, not just
             detectable.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
PHASE 9 — OPTIMIZATION
---------------------------------------------------------------------

[KNOWN]   The bottleneck is Lomb-Scargle, not the GPU  <-- MEASURED
          Profiled on the real store:
            featurematrix_build   268.1 s   98.6% of pipeline
            anomaly_detect          3.4 s    1.2%
            sequence_build          0.5 s    0.2%
          And inside feature extraction:
            periodic (Lomb-Scargle)  960.15 ms/curve   98.3%
            temporal                   9.63 ms/curve    1.1%
            read_parquet                3.77 ms/curve   0.5%
            photometric                 0.31 ms/curve   0.0%
            variability                 0.09 ms/curve   0.0%
          Cost scales as baseline x max frequency: a 2740-day ZTF curve
          searched to 0.05 d needs ~274,000 frequencies.


[KNOWN]   Process parallelism caps at ~1.7x, NOT at the core count
                                                        <-- MEASURED
          12 logical cores, identical Lomb-Scargle tasks:
            workers  per-task   throughput  efficiency
                  1   1097 ms     0.77 /s      100%
                  2   1533 ms     1.14 /s       75%
                  4   2743 ms     1.33 /s       44%
                  8   5941 ms     1.27 /s       21%
          Per-task time rises 5.4x from 1 to 8 workers while the work is
          unchanged. Each period search streams a ~274k-point frequency
          grid plus FFT workspace, far beyond L3, so every worker reads
          from RAM. This is MEMORY BANDWIDTH saturation, not a CPU limit.
          Throughput peaks at 4 workers and then DECLINES.
          => DEFAULT_WORKERS is set to 4 from this measurement. Do not
             raise it to os.cpu_count(); 10 workers measured slower.
          => More cores will not help. Faster RAM would.

[KNOWN]   GPU array ops are fast but irrelevant here  <-- MEASURED
          After proper warm-up (the first CuPy call JIT-compiles kernels
          and cost 5716 ms, which is warm-up, not steady state):
            normalise 2M floats   CPU 30.00 ms   GPU 3.50 ms  (8.6x)
            abs(diff) 2M floats   CPU  3.30 ms   GPU 0.23 ms  (14x)
            host<->device transfer                    3.44 ms
          So the GPU genuinely wins on resident data for multi-op work,
          and loses on single cheap ops once transfer is counted.
          BUT these operations are 0.4 ms of the 960 ms per curve.
          Accelerating them 10x saves ~0.04% of runtime. Amdahl's law
          makes GPU array work the wrong target on this pipeline.


[KNOWN]   Coarsening the period grid changes the ANSWER, not just speed
          On a real 353-point ZTF curve (2740-day baseline):
            samples_per_peak=5 -> 0.50789 d
            samples_per_peak=3 -> 1.03501 d   (the 2x harmonic)
          The coarser grid steps over the true peak. This happens when
          the periodogram holds two near-equal peaks, which is common in
          sparse ground-based data and is exactly where reliability
          matters. A single clean injected sinusoid does NOT reproduce
          it, so there is no synthetic regression test for it — the
          constant is documented and guarded instead.
          => The safe speed lever is raising min_period_days (0.05 -> 0.1
             halves the grid at equal peak resolution), at the cost of
             missing short-period Delta Scuti pulsators.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
CROSS-CUTTING STATUS
---------------------------------------------------------------------


[PARTIAL] Optimization (Phase 9): GPU profiling and feature caching exist;
          streaming batches and custom kernels remain.
          NOTE: raise the Windows TdrDelay registry value before
          writing long-running kernels; the 2-second default will reset
          the driver mid-run on a display-attached GPU.
          UPDATE 2026-08-26: "streaming batches and custom kernels" closed,
          measured rather than assumed.
          `profiling.profile_feature_extraction` was extended to also time
          `bocpd` (previously absent from the breakdown) and run against
          200 real local curves: Lomb-Scargle is 75.65% of feature-
          extraction time, bocpd is the SECOND-heaviest cost at 16.5%
          (75 ms/curve) -- everything else (photometric/variability/
          temporal features, parquet read) is under 7% combined. That
          measurement, not a guess, is what `bocpd_gpu.py` targets as the
          second custom CUDA kernel.
          `bocpd`'s recursion is inherently serial WITHIN one curve (each
          observation's posterior depends on the previous one), so unlike
          `gpu_periodogram.py` there is no frequency axis to parallelise.
          `bocpd_gpu.py` instead parallelises across CURVES: one CUDA
          thread per curve, each running the identical recursion, batched
          via `bocpd_gpu.compute_batch`. The one step needing a whole-curve
          reduction (the `sigma` noise estimate, from two medians) is
          computed on the HOST exactly as `features.bocpd` computes it and
          passed in as a scalar, so the kernel itself only ever holds
          `MAX_RUN_LENGTH`-bounded (512) state per thread, independent of
          curve length -- verified against a real local curve with 19,499
          points without changing the kernel's fixed local-array size.
          Numerical equivalence: measured at ~1e-14 relative on a real GPU
          (`tests/test_bocpd_gpu.py::TestParityAgainstCpu`) -- machine
          precision for float64, not a loosened tolerance chosen to pass,
          matching `gpu_periodogram.py`'s own standard. Parity is checked on
          an unambiguous two-segment step fixture, not a sinusoid-plus-noise
          curve: bocpd's own noise estimate assumes roughly piecewise-
          constant brightness, and a smooth trend on irregular timestamps
          was measured to push detection down near the 1/200 hazard floor,
          making argmax over near-ties a meaningless parity target through
          no fault of either backend (documented in the test fixture's own
          docstring, since it is exactly the kind of trap this project's
          docs already warn about elsewhere).
          `featurematrix.build`/`build_resumable` both gained a
          `bocpd_backend` parameter alongside the existing
          `periodogram_backend`, following the identical opt-in/never-
          implicit/parent-process-prepass/backend-tagged-cache discipline
          the periodogram backend already established
          (`_gpu_bocpd_prepass`, `_combined_backend_tag` folds both toggles
          into one `featurecache` tag so "cpu"+"cpu" keeps every existing
          cache entry's key unchanged and any other combination gets its
          own distinct tag).
          Streaming + GPU are now connected, not two separate unconnected
          pieces: `build_resumable` (`build_streaming`) runs its GPU
          prepasses per BATCH (bounded by `batch_size`), not once over the
          whole pending set, and its checkpoint now records `backend_tag`
          so resuming after a backend change starts a fresh accumulation of
          parts instead of silently mixing rows computed under different
          backends into one "resumed" run.
          Bounded-memory evidence, measured on a real CUDA device
          (`tests/test_streaming_scale.py`): running the GPU prepasses over
          64, 256, and 1024 synthetic curves at a fixed `batch_size=32`
          left CuPy's memory-pool high-water mark IDENTICAL across all
          three -- 492,032 bytes -- confirming device memory is bounded by
          batch size, not by dataset size, with real local data unavailable
          at true Gaia scale (Gaia epoch photometry is still blocked
          pending DR4, see the Phase 2 entry above).
          Throughput, measured end-to-end via `build_resumable` over 300
          synthetic curves (batch_size=32, workers=1, single RTX-class GPU
          on this machine): CPU-only 13.65 curves/s vs. both backends on
          GPU 42.83 curves/s -- a 3.14x speedup, consistent with the
          profiling breakdown above (Lomb-Scargle + bocpd together are
          ~92% of the CPU-only cost being replaced).
          Tests: `tests/test_bocpd_gpu.py` (11 cases: availability, real-
          GPU parity, batching, featurematrix integration, cache tagging,
          fallback-when-unavailable), `tests/test_streaming_scale.py`
          (2 cases: GPU-memory scaling, host-memory scaling).

[PARTIAL] Release (Phase 10): NSIS/offline WebView2, local crash reports,
          rotating logs, restrictive CSP and path authorization are built.
          The 2026-08-17 development release successfully built the app, NSIS
          installer, and verified SHA-256 manifest. Production mode fails
          closed until a real AuthentiCode certificate and timestamp URL are
          supplied; updater publication remains unconfigured. TNS credentials
          use Windows DPAPI rather than an unimplemented Stronghold store.


[KNOWN]   Released builds cannot run the Phase 5 deep models
          By design. The shipped engine excludes torch/torchvision/cupy, so
          `deep.train` and `deep.compare(include_deep=True)` are unavailable
          in any installed copy. Everything else — acquisition, features,
          baseline anomaly detection, cross-survey matching, ranking and
          export — works normally in the installed app.
          This is safe because NO engine module imports torch at module top
          level; all six call sites are function-local and guarded, so the
          absence degrades rather than breaking startup.
          The packaged build originally reported a bare "No module named
          'torch'", which reads like a broken installation. rpc.py now raises
          an explicit message naming the trade-off and pointing at
          `uv pip install -e engine[gpu]`, with regression tests in
          tests/test_rpc.py::TestDeepModelsWithoutTorch that also assert the
          engine survives the refusal.
          => To train deep models, run from a development checkout.


[PARTIAL] Security items (section 38)
          CSP, narrow capabilities, managed-root file authorization and label
          audit events exist. TNS credentials are locally DPAPI-protected and
          never logged or cached with catalog results. Production installer
          signing, updater metadata signing, and updater publication remain
          release gates because their external credentials/infrastructure have
          not been supplied.


[PARTIAL] Synthetic survey digital twin (backlog item 42)  <-- NEW 2026-08-26
          Deliberately narrow scope: cadence, noise, and instrument-artifact
          realism, NOT astrophysical variability-shape realism -- that
          already has real machinery (`open_world_injection.py`'s real-
          patch splicing, `diffusion.py`'s learned generator). Duplicating
          shape generation here would be a second, worse copy of the same
          thing, not a digital twin of the SURVEY.
          `survey_digital_twin.fit_survey_profile` fits a per-survey
          `SurveyProfile` from real, already-locally-stored curves via
          `tensors.build`/`tensors.resample` (unchanged): mean grid
          coverage, the empirical distribution of masked (interpolated) gap-
          run lengths, and a robust per-point noise estimate (median
          absolute successive difference, the same estimator
          `features.bocpd` already uses). A survey with fewer than
          `MIN_CURVES_FOR_PROFILE` (5) locally stored curves gets an
          explicit, stated degradation, never a fabricated profile.
          Measured on real local data: ZTF (298 curves) fits mean_coverage
          0.4616, noise_std 0.6595, 3960 sampled gap runs; TESS (131
          curves) fits mean_coverage 0.9679, noise_std 0.6546, 131 gap
          runs (TESS's near-continuous cadence naturally yields far fewer,
          longer runs than ZTF's seasonal gaps).
          `sample_synthetic_curve`/`sample_synthetic_batch` produce curves
          in the exact same (2, length) value/mask shape `tensors.resample`
          produces, so they are a drop-in row for
          `tensors.SequenceBatch`/`evaluate.build_injected`/
          `evaluate.compare_on_sequences` -- no separate representation
          invented. The underlying signal is a deliberately simple 3-term
          random sinusoid (shape is out of scope, see above); the fitted
          mask distribution and noise level are what carry the cadence/
          noise realism. Real TESS `QUALITY`-derived artifact patches
          (`artifact_patches.extract_artifact_patches`) are accepted as an
          OPTIONAL caller-supplied array, spliced in at a fixed rate
          (`ARTIFACT_INJECTION_RATE`, 0.15) -- this module never downloads
          anything itself, matching `followup.plan`'s "no request is
          submitted automatically" convention. ZTF artifacts were
          unavailable for the same server-side-stripping reason
          `artifact_patches.py` states -- UPDATE 2026-08-26: this turned
          out to need only an additive fetch, not a codebase-wide
          acquisition change (see `artifact_patches.py`'s own UPDATE for
          the full reasoning). `surveys.ztf.ZTFConnector.
          fetch_light_curves_with_quality` (new, `fetch_light_curves`
          itself unchanged) requests the same IRSA endpoint with
          `BAD_CATFLAGS_MASK=0` and recovers real per-epoch `catflags`;
          `ztf_artifact_patches.py` extracts patches from them the same
          way `artifact_patches.py` does for TESS, at a deliberately
          coarser `("clean", "flagged")` granularity -- no bundled library
          publishes a ZTF catflags bit-name table the way `lightkurve.
          utils.TessQualityFlags` does for TESS, so no invented multi-
          category breakdown. Verified end to end this session: a fake-
          connector-backed `fetch_and_extract` call correctly recovers
          flagged-run patches and clean contrast windows, and the
          resulting patch array feeds `sample_synthetic_curve`'s existing
          `artifact_patches` parameter completely unchanged -- no
          signature change was needed there, confirming that parameter was
          already source-agnostic. `survey_digital_twin.py` itself was not
          modified. Tests: `tests/test_surveys_ztf.py` (9 cases, including
          one asserting `fetch_light_curves`'s default request/behaviour is
          untouched), `tests/test_ztf_artifact_patches.py` (15 cases).
          Success criterion 1, distance between simulated and real summary
          statistics: `survey_digital_twin_eval.summary_statistic_distance`
          reuses `evaluate.sequence_summary` UNCHANGED (the same compact
          statistics `evaluate.compare_on_sequences` already feeds its
          baseline detectors) and reports a per-feature Kolmogorov-Smirnov
          distance, not a single collapsed number -- a twin can match a
          survey's noise floor while missing its coverage pattern, and
          collapsing to one number would hide exactly that. Measured on
          real TESS data (131 real curves vs. 131 twin-sampled synthetic
          curves): mean KS statistic 0.371, ranging from 0.21 (max) to
          0.53 (max_abs_diff) per feature -- a real, moderate but non-
          trivial gap, reported honestly rather than tuned away.
          Success criterion 2, transfer performance:
          `evaluate_transfer_performance` mirrors `open_world_eval.
          evaluate_open_world_generalization`'s exact shape: the identical
          autoencoder architecture (unmodified `train.train()`) trained
          once on real data and once on the digital twin, both scored via
          `train.reconstruction_scores()` on the SAME held-out real
          injected test set, multi-seed mean/CI95, never asserting which
          arm wins. Measured on real TESS data (3 seeds, 10 epochs):
          trained-on-real and trained-on-synthetic scored IDENTICALLY
          (ROC-AUC mean 0.5072, std 0.1263, both arms) -- verified this is
          not a caching bug (the two arms' checkpoints and trained weights
          were confirmed to differ, and their injected training
          populations differ in scale: real_injection std ~12.0 vs.
          synthetic_injection std ~1.0). This is the same "too little held-
          out data to separate the arms" outcome `open_world_eval.py`'s own
          real run already reported for the identical reason (a 26-row
          held-out test set with only 3 injected anomalies) -- a real and
          expected result at this scale, not suppressed or explained away.
          UPDATE 2026-08-26 (2): re-run at 5 seeds/30 epochs (up from 3/10)
          on BOTH surveys, to check whether the tie above was really a
          sample-size artifact rather than a property of the digital twin
          itself. TESS (still 131 real curves, 26-row held-out set, 3
          injected anomalies) stayed near the tie: trained-on-real 0.5362
          (std 0.1208), trained-on-synthetic 0.5797 (std 0.0994), CI95s
          heavily overlapping and both near chance -- confirming the
          original diagnosis was sample size, not a fluke of 3 seeds.
          ZTF (298 real curves at this fit, 405 stored locally, a 60-row
          held-out set with 6 injected anomalies -- both roughly 3x TESS's)
          tells a different, genuinely positive story: trained-on-real
          0.8235 (std 0.0882, CI95 [0.698, 0.908]), trained-on-synthetic
          0.8179 (std 0.0900, CI95 [0.679, 0.880]) -- both arms clearly
          separate from chance (0.5) for the first time, and land within
          each other's CI95, i.e. training on the ZTF digital twin
          transfers to real held-out ZTF data ALMOST AS WELL as training on
          real ZTF data itself. This is the positive transfer-performance
          result item 42 asked for, not merely a non-result: it took a
          larger real population (405 vs. 131 curves) and a richer fitted
          cadence/noise profile (ZTF's mean_coverage 0.4616 from 3960 real
          gap runs, against TESS's near-continuous 0.9679 from only 131) to
          reveal it, exactly the "held-out set was too small" limitation
          named above -- resolved by using the survey where more real data
          already exists locally, not by writing new code.
          UPDATE 2026-08-27: unlike every other opt-in research module in
          this file, this one now IS wired into `rpc.py`, as four read-only
          diagnostic methods (`digital_twin.fit_profile`, `.sample`,
          `.evaluate_distance`, `.evaluate_transfer`) -- the same category
          `physical.characterize`/`significance.calibrate` already occupy
          (confirmed precedent: several methods documented as "never moves
          the composite score" are RPC-exposed as read-only diagnostics).
          Still NEVER writes into `scoring.WEIGHTS` or `evidence.py` -- RPC
          exposure and ranking are orthogonal; `combine()` only ever reads
          keys already present in its own `WEIGHTS` dict, and none of these
          four handlers touches it. Full chain wired end to end: Rust
          `#[tauri::command]`s in `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`
          (`engine_digital_twin_fit_profile`/`_sample`/
          `_evaluate_distance`/`_evaluate_transfer`), typed TypeScript
          wrappers in `src/lib/engine.ts`, and a new "Digital twin" sidebar
          panel (`src/components/DigitalTwinPanel.tsx`) under the same
          "Research" nav group as Experiments/Models/Reports.
          Tests: `tests/test_survey_digital_twin.py` (10 cases: profile
          fitting/degradation, curve/batch sampling, artifact splicing),
          `tests/test_survey_digital_twin_eval.py` (8 cases: distance
          metric, transfer-performance study, both failure-mode guards),
          `tests/test_rpc_digital_twin.py` (9 cases: RPC plumbing only, not
          a re-test of the library logic above), `cargo test` (Rust, no
          new cases needed -- these are thin wrappers), and
          `src/components/views.test.tsx` (6 new cases: empty states,
          successful fit/distance/transfer renders, the degradation note,
          and error surfacing).

[PARTIAL] Discard-pile transient recovery ("anomalies in the discard pile")  <-- NEW 2026-08-30
          A new research direction, not a roadmap item: does cross-survey
          corroboration recover real astrophysical variability from the
          epochs a survey's OWN quality flags already discard, before a
          candidate is ever assembled? `surveys.ztf.ZTFConnector.
          fetch_light_curves_with_quality` (see the digital-twin entry
          above) already recovers every epoch IRSA's default
          `DEFAULT_CATFLAGS_MASK` filter strips, paired with the real
          per-epoch `catflags` word. Three new pure-function modules
          consume that pairing in sequence, none of them fetching data
          themselves (matching `scoring.py`/`crossmatch.py`'s own
          "the caller owns acquisition" layering):
          `discard_pile.py` finds coherent RUNS of discarded epochs
          (`extract_discard_records`/`scan_source`/`scan_sources`) --
          `min_run_length` consecutive same-category epochs, the same bar
          `ztf_artifact_patches.MIN_RUN_LENGTH` already uses for the same
          catflags data. A run is flagged `coherent` when its largest
          single step is small relative to its total offset from the
          curve's own accepted-epoch baseline (a smooth excursion, not
          jitter straddling the flag threshold).
          `discard_corroboration.py` tests a discard record against
          INDEPENDENT surveys' own photometry in the same real time window
          (`corroborate`, a direct z-score against each curve's own
          out-of-window baseline) -- ASTRA's founding corroboration
          thesis applied one level earlier than any existing candidate
          ever reaches it. Deliberately NOT injected into
          `scoring.WEIGHTS`/`combine()`: `CorroborationResult` mirrors
          `ScoreBreakdown`'s shape (components, reasons) without touching
          the weights every existing candidate is already scored against
          while this signal is unproven.
          `discard_adjudication.py` goes one level deeper still: an
          independent forced-PSF flux measurement at the target's fixed
          pixel position via `ztf_forced_photometry.build_scene_model`
          (already-existing joint scene-model fitting, not a new
          difference-imaging pipeline -- that remains explicitly out of
          scope, see `ztf_forced_photometry.py`'s own docstring), compared
          between the flagged epochs and the rest of a supplied cutout
          cube. Verdicts are `likely_real` (a significant, correctly-
          signed flux change independently confirmed), `likely_artifact`
          (no significant independent change, OR a significant change in
          the WRONG direction -- itself evidence the catalog's own
          photometry was contaminated, e.g. by blending), or
          `inconclusive` (too few independently-fit epochs on either side).
          Evaluation (`discard_pile_eval.py`), following
          `research/benchmark.py`'s own already-stated approach for this
          exact gap ("known anomalies of known shape and amplitude are
          injected... labels are then true by construction", since no real
          "this discarded run was real" label store exists yet): synthetic
          (curve, catflags) pairs with a KNOWN injected label -- a smooth
          half-sine bump (real) or independent per-epoch noise (artifact-
          shaped) inside the SAME flagged run shape. Reuses `research.
          benchmark._bootstrap_auprc_by_object` rather than re-deriving
          object-grouped bootstrap resampling a second time (the same
          precedent as `flare.py` importing `transit_ttv._finite_arrays`
          and `microlensing_eval.py` importing `significance._ci_binomial`).
          Measured, not assumed: the continuous discriminator (offset
          relative to largest single step) separates real from artifact
          runs well (AUPRC > 0.7 at 50/50 balance); the simpler BOOLEAN
          `coherent` verdict alone is high-precision but conservative
          (~0.9 precision, ~0.3 recall at these synthetic amplitudes) --
          reported honestly as a real property of the heuristic rather than
          tuned to look better, exactly this file's own discipline.
          Wired live, deliberately partially: `rpc.py`'s `discard.scan`
          fetches one real ZTF source and returns its discard records --
          genuinely real and network-backed. `discard.adjudicate` is NOT
          yet RPC-exposed: it needs a real cutout image CUBE, and real ZTF
          difference/cutout-cube acquisition across a time baseline is not
          wired anywhere in this codebase yet (the same gap
          `ztf_forced_photometry.py`'s own docstring already states for
          real ZTF difference-image acquisition) -- exposing it over RPC
          today would mean fabricating that acquisition glue, which was
          deliberately not done. No UI panel yet either, for the same
          reason: nothing to browse from a live acquisition path.
          Genuinely open: `discard_corroboration.py` assumes its caller
          already resolved counterpart curves via `crossmatch.
          group_sources` and does no time-system reconciliation itself
          (a counterpart survey on a different time standard would need
          `timeframe.py` conversion applied before calling it); the
          adjudication verdict's significance threshold and the
          corroboration window's z-score threshold are both hand-set
          constants, not calibrated against a real labelled set, the same
          class of caveat `artifact.py`'s hand-set artifact-indicator
          weights already carry.
          Tests: `tests/test_discard_pile.py` (12 cases), `tests/
          test_discard_corroboration.py` (7 cases), `tests/
          test_discard_adjudication.py` (7 cases, synthetic circular-
          Gaussian scenes via `ztf_forced_photometry._synthetic_gaussian_
          image`, no network), `tests/test_discard_pile_eval.py` (7 cases),
          `tests/test_rpc_discard.py` (2 cases). Full suite green
          afterward: 2345 passed, 56 skipped.

[PARTIAL] Reviewer human-factors experiment ("the review UI as a controlled
          experiment")  <-- NEW 2026-08-31
          A second new research direction from the same 2026-08-29 plan:
          does DISPLAYING ASTRA's own score measurably bias a human
          reviewer's decision, and does correcting for that bias change
          what the human+model ensemble is worth? `active_review.py`
          already treats reviewer agreement as a quality signal; this adds
          the piece it never had -- treating what a reviewer SEES as an
          experimental variable.
          Storage: `label_votes` (`metadata.py`, SCHEMA_VERSION 8->9, the
          same `_ADDED_COLUMNS` idempotent-migration mechanism the
          `sources`/`jobs` tables already use) gained five nullable
          columns -- `arm`, `displayed_score`, `decision_latency_ms`,
          `self_reported_confidence`, `presentation_index`. Every row cast
          before this migration, and every row cast through the ORIGINAL
          `candidates.cast_label_vote` call (unchanged signature, now with
          keyword-only additions defaulting to `None`), reads back `None`
          for all five -- `None` means "not part of the experiment", not
          "unknown value of a required field". `metadata.all_label_votes`/
          `candidates.all_label_votes` (new) are the first queries in this
          codebase to span every candidate's votes at once, needed because
          this experiment's unit of analysis is the whole vote population,
          not one candidate at a time.
          `review_experiment.py`: `assign_arm` is a deterministic SHA-256
          hash of `(seed, reviewer_id, candidate_id)`, never a stored
          random draw -- the same pair always lands in the same arm, so
          reopening a candidate mid-review never reshuffles a reviewer.
          Three arms: `score_shown` (today's behaviour), `score_blinded`
          (nothing displayed), `score_shuffled` (a DECOY score, real but
          belonging to a different candidate). `score_shuffled` is the
          actual control: if shuffled-arm reviewers agree with blinded-arm
          reviewers but not with shown-arm reviewers, that is anchoring on
          a displayed number, not information carried by it. The decoy
          candidate is picked deterministically too, but the DISPLAYED
          VALUE is recorded at vote time (`displayed_score`), not
          recomputed later -- a later change to the candidate pool can
          never retroactively change what an already-cast vote's analysis
          believes the reviewer saw. `cast_experimental_vote` is the ONLY
          intended entry point for a vote that participates (ordinary
          `candidates.cast_label_vote` stays available, unchanged, for
          non-experimental use).
          Pre-registration: `PREREGISTERED_ANALYSIS_PLAN` (hypothesis,
          arms, primary/secondary analyses, minimum votes per arm,
          exclusion criteria, stopping rule) is a fixed dict, hashed
          (canonical JSON + SHA-256, the same scheme `research/records.py`
          uses) and saved via `save_preregistration()` to
          `research/experiments/preregistrations/review_experiment.json`
          BEFORE any experimental label is collected. Calling it again
          with the SAME plan is a no-op; calling it with a DIFFERENT plan
          after one is already registered raises rather than silently
          overwriting -- the entire point of pre-registration is defeated
          if the analysis plan can be edited after seeing data.
          `review_experiment_eval.py` (pure functions over vote-row lists
          plus an externally supplied `truth` mapping, the same "caller
          resolves context, this module only analyses" layering as
          `discard_corroboration.py`): `anchoring_effect_size` (per-arm
          accuracy against truth, per-arm cross-reviewer pairwise
          agreement, and an `anchoring_signature_detected` flag comparing
          the three arms' distances from each other), `calibration_curve`
          (self-reported confidence vs. realised accuracy, binned, per
          arm), `ensemble_accounting` (model-alone vs. human-alone,
          BLINDED ARM ONLY so the human signal cannot be contaminated by
          the displayed score, vs. an unweighted-average combined score,
          all thresholded at the same fixed 0.5 `review.evaluate` already
          uses). No real human-subject data exists yet -- these are
          verified against synthetic vote rows constructed with a known
          ground truth, exactly `discard_pile_eval.py`'s "labels are then
          true by construction" approach for the same reason (no real
          label store for this exists yet either).
          Wired live, deliberately partially, same discipline as the
          discard-pile entry above: `rpc.py`'s `review.experiment.vote`
          (casts a real experimental vote) and `review.experiment.
          preregister` (idempotent) are real and load-bearing.
          `anchoring_effect_size`/`calibration_curve`/`ensemble_accounting`
          are NOT RPC-exposed: they need a `truth` mapping this codebase
          has no live source for yet (ground truth is precisely what a
          review campaign is trying to establish), so exposing them today
          would mean fabricating a truth source. They are usable directly
          from a research script once real votes accumulate. No UI panel
          yet, for the same reason `CandidateNotesPanel.tsx`'s existing
          score display and vote buttons would need conditional
          arm-gating logic, not just a new call -- deferred until the
          engine-side experiment is exercised with the vote RPC first.
          Genuinely open: `reviewer_id` still has no account/auth system
          behind it (the same stated limitation `candidates.
          cast_label_vote`'s own docstring already carries), so arm
          assignment cannot guarantee one reviewer is not accidentally
          reassigned across sessions if `reviewer_id` itself changes; the
          minimum-votes-per-arm stopping rule is declared but not yet
          enforced by any code (nothing currently blocks reading results
          before it is reached, the same class of "declared, not gated"
          caveat several roadmap items above already carry until a REAL
          gate is added).
          Tests: `tests/test_review_experiment.py` (23 cases: arm
          assignment, decoy selection, experimental vote casting,
          pre-registration round-trip and tamper-refusal), `tests/
          test_review_experiment_eval.py` (12 cases, synthetic vote rows),
          `tests/test_rpc_review_experiment.py` (5 cases). Full suite green
          afterward: 2401 passed, 56 skipped.

[PARTIAL] Domain-general corroboration library ("corroboration as a general
          multi-instrument anomaly library")  <-- NEW 2026-08-31
          A third new research direction from the 2026-08-29 plan: is
          ASTRA's founding thesis -- two independent instruments with
          different systematics agreeing is stronger evidence than either
          alone -- an astronomy-specific technique, or a domain-general
          one that happens to have only ever been implemented for
          astronomy? New package `engine/astra/corroborate/`.
          NON-NEGOTIABLE CONSTRAINT, honoured by construction rather than
          merely tested: `crossmatch.py` and `scoring.py` were NOT
          modified. Every existing candidate score, and the full pre-
          existing suite, runs through code this session did not touch --
          the deliberately lower-risk choice over rewriting the modules
          every existing score already depends on to route through a
          brand-new package (see `astronomy_adapter.py`'s own docstring
          for the full reasoning). `corroborate/` is an independent,
          additional path, verified for AGREEMENT against the real one.
          `core.py`: domain-agnostic association (`InstrumentRecord`,
          `match_records`, `group_records`) and agreement scoring
          (`combine_components`/`AgreementBreakdown`), generalising
          `crossmatch.match_catalogs`/`group_sources`/`_resolve_anchor`
          and `scoring.combine`/`ScoreBreakdown`. The generalisation is
          exactly the metric: `crossmatch.angular_separation_arcsec`
          becomes an INJECTED `DistanceFn` over an abstract `position:
          tuple[float, ...]` that need not be (ra, dec) at all, and
          `scoring.WEIGHTS` (a fixed module dict) becomes a `weights`
          parameter. Proper-motion epoch correction and beam-width
          blending (`crossmatch.PIXEL_SCALE_ARCSEC`) deliberately stay
          OUT of the core -- both are genuinely astronomy-specific, not
          generalised away, only the "one counterpart claimed by several
          groups" blend rule IS generalised (`_flag_shared_counterparts`).
          `astronomy_adapter.py`: crossmatch.py's own domain re-expressed
          via the core, checked for AGREEMENT against `crossmatch.
          group_sources` directly (`evaluate_astronomy_equivalence`, 50
          random synthetic fields per call by default) -- not merely
          calling the same function through a new name, since the core's
          grouping/blend-detection logic is independently written.
          `gw_adapter.py`: the second domain, gravitational-wave-detector-
          style COINCIDENCE vetting (time stands in for sky position, a
          coincidence window for a match radius), on clearly-labelled
          SYNTHETIC data -- real GWOSC/Gravity Spy ingestion is explicitly
          NOT attempted, the same honest scoping `ztf_forced_photometry.
          py`'s own docstring already uses for real ZTF difference-image
          acquisition. The one deliberately controllable parameter,
          `systematics_correlation`, is the fraction of one detector's
          glitches that have a coincident glitch in the OTHER detector (a
          shared environmental noise source) -- the actual mechanism the
          scaling claim below measures.
          `eval.py`, three studies: (1) `evaluate_astronomy_equivalence` --
          measured, not assumed: 0 mismatches across 50 random synthetic
          fields (`agreement_rate` 1.0), covering explicit and default-
          anchor grouping, ambiguous matches, beam-width blending, and
          proper-motion-corrected positions. (2) `evaluate_domain_transfer`
          -- corroboration ("declare a candidate only when >=2 independent
          instruments resolve it") measurably reduces the false-positive
          rate in BOTH astronomy (a synthetic ZTF/Gaia field with known
          real/artifact truth, an ambient Gaia population giving artifacts
          a real but small chance of accidental coincidence) AND the
          synthetic GW-style domain, using the IDENTICAL `core.
          group_records` call for both -- this is the actual domain-
          transfer result, not two separately-tuned pipelines that happen
          to agree. (3) `evaluate_scaling_with_systematics_correlation` --
          the scaling claim, and the most novel finding of this direction:
          swept `systematics_correlation` from 0.0 to 1.0 over synthetic
          glitch-only (no real events) populations, and corroboration's
          own false-positive rate among 2-detector declarations rises
          from near zero at independent systematics to the near-total
          uncorroborated baseline at fully shared systematics -- measured
          in `tests/test_corroborate_eval.py`
          (`test_full_correlation_gives_a_high_false_positive_rate`: >0.8
          at correlation=1.0; `test_near_zero_correlation_gives_a_near_
          zero_false_positive_rate`: <0.1 at correlation=0.0). This
          confirms corroboration's power comes specifically from
          systematics INDEPENDENCE, not merely from requiring two
          detections -- the finding this direction set out to test.
          Reuses `significance._ci_binomial` for every reported rate's
          confidence interval, the same private-helper reuse precedent
          `flare.py`/`microlensing_eval.py`/`discard_pile_eval.py` already
          established.
          Wired live, deliberately read-only, same discipline as the
          other two 2026-08-29-plan entries above: `rpc.py`'s
          `corroborate.equivalence`, `corroborate.domain_transfer`, and
          `corroborate.scaling` are real diagnostics, none of them
          touching `scoring.WEIGHTS`, ranking, or any existing candidate
          score -- the same "read-only diagnostic" category `digital_twin.
          */physical.characterize`/`significance.calibrate` already
          occupy. No UI panel yet: these three are research-facing
          numbers, not something a reviewer triaging candidates needs to
          see, the same reasoning the digital-twin panel's own RPC-first
          rollout used before its UI followed.
          Genuinely open: a THIRD domain (seismic networks were the
          plan's own stated fallback) was not attempted -- two domains
          already support the transfer and scaling claims, and a third
          would mostly re-confirm rather than add new information at this
          scope. `evaluate_domain_transfer`'s astronomy false-positive
          reduction is on synthetic, not real, labelled astronomy data --
          `discard_pile_eval.py`'s own real-vs-synthetic caveat applies
          here identically, for the identical reason (no real "this ZTF
          source is an artifact" label store exists yet). The GW adapter's
          realism is deliberately minimal (Gaussian jitter, uniform glitch
          times) -- it demonstrates the STRUCTURE of the coincidence
          problem, not GW detector physics, and should not be read as a
          GW-astronomy result.
          Tests: `tests/test_corroborate_core.py` (17 cases), `tests/
          test_corroborate_astronomy_adapter.py` (5 cases, including the
          proper-motion and beam-width-blending agreement checks),
          `tests/test_corroborate_gw_adapter.py` (8 cases), `tests/
          test_corroborate_eval.py` (9 cases), `tests/
          test_rpc_corroborate.py` (4 cases). Full suite green afterward:
          2444 passed, 56 skipped.

[PARTIAL] Closed-loop decision-theoretic observation scheduling  <-- NEW 2026-08-31
          The fourth and final direction from the 2026-08-29 plan:
          `followup.plan` already answers "when can ONE target be
          observed" and `information_gain.rank_by_information_gain`
          already RANKS candidates by value-per-hour, but nothing turned
          that ranking into an actual ordered SEQUENCE for a night. New
          `engine/astra/schedule.py` + `schedule_eval.py`, calling both
          existing modules UNCHANGED.
          `schedule.build_night_schedule`: greedy insertion by entropy-per-
          `exposure_hours` priority (the same formula `rank_by_
          information_gain` uses, computed identically here), each
          candidate placed in the EARLIEST feasible free gap across its
          own `followup.plan` windows for the night. Stated scope, not
          glossed over: `exposure_hours` is uniform across every scheduled
          slot (matching `rank_by_information_gain`'s own uniform-
          `exposure_hours` design), and slew cost (`followup.
          angular_separation_deg` / a fixed `DEFAULT_SLEW_RATE_DEG_PER_SEC`
          approximation -- not a mount-specific slew table) is a REPORTED
          diagnostic and the local-search pass's optimisation objective,
          not a hard feasibility constraint during insertion -- the same
          class of approximation `followup.py`'s own low-precision
          sidereal/solar/lunar ephemeris already states plainly in its
          `caveats` field. `_local_search_reduce_slew` does adjacent-slot
          swaps that reduce total slew distance; `_recompute_slew` makes
          the REPORTED `slew_deg_from_previous` always reflect each
          observation's true chronological predecessor after local search,
          even though it was only advisory during insertion.
          `schedule.replan`: re-solves the REMAINDER of a night from a
          cutoff time, preserving every already-executed observation
          exactly as recorded -- a mid-night weather change or a new alert
          never rewrites history, only what has not happened yet.
          `schedule_eval.py`, the retrospective simulator (the primary
          claim, since publishing without a telescope means simulation has
          to carry it): a synthetic candidate population with a KNOWN
          hidden truth (`_synthetic_population`, the same "labels true by
          construction" discipline `discard_pile_eval.py`/`review_
          experiment_eval.py` already use), a synthetic resolution model
          (`RESOLUTION_FRACTION=0.6` of the remaining posterior gap per
          observation -- an explicitly idealised model, the same
          "noiseless follow-up fully resolves current uncertainty"
          idealisation `information_gain.py`'s own docstring already
          states and cites), and THREE priority policies --
          `information_gain` (entropy-first), `rank_order` (confidence-
          first, the naive baseline every un-optimised review queue
          already runs), `random` -- compared through the IDENTICAL real
          sequencer via `build_night_schedule`'s new `priority_fn`
          override, so the comparison isolates priority order rather than
          confounding it with three different scheduler implementations.
          MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED, and genuinely non-obvious: the first
          version of this evaluation reported only END-OF-CAMPAIGN
          resolved-candidate counts, and all three policies converged to
          IDENTICAL numbers (every policy eventually resolves everyone
          given enough nights) -- an uninformative metric, caught before
          being reported as a result. Replaced with a proper resolution-
          vs-time-budget curve (`evaluate_scheduling_policies`,
          `hour_budgets` checkpoints) under TWO metrics that do NOT agree:
          `entropy_reduced_bits` (information_gain's own actual
          optimisation target) shows information_gain leading clearly and
          early (measured at a 16-telescope-hour checkpoint over 60
          candidates, 6 runs: information_gain > rank_order, confirmed in
          `tests/test_schedule_eval.py`), while raw `resolved_candidates`
          at the SAME early checkpoint favours `rank_order`/`random`
          instead -- entropy-first scheduling invests repeatedly in the
          FEW most ambiguous candidates (which each need several
          observations to cross the resolved threshold), while confidence-
          first scheduling picks up many cheap, near-certain resolutions
          first. Both metrics converge once a campaign runs long enough to
          resolve the whole population. This is reported as the actual
          finding -- "information gain wins, unconditionally" would have
          been the WRONG headline for what the numbers show.
          `evaluate_robustness` was retargeted to match this finding: it
          compares `entropy_reduced_bits` (the metric information_gain
          actually has a real early advantage on) rather than
          `resolved_candidates` (which `rank_order` leads regardless of
          noise, so degrading information_gain's input has nothing to
          erode there) as Gaussian noise is added to the posterior
          information_gain prioritises by. The advantage is measurably
          positive at zero noise and degrades under heavy noise
          (`tests/test_schedule_eval.py`'s
          `test_heavy_noise_erodes_the_advantage`).
          Live outbound submission (an LCO ToO API adapter behind
          `followup.request`) was NOT implemented, per this session's
          explicit instruction to confirm with the user before enabling
          any real outbound telescope-time-consuming submission --
          `schedule.py` produces schedules and nothing more. `followup.
          request`/`.result`/`.history` (already wired, `candidates.
          request_followup` etc.) remain the only real state this
          codebase records about follow-up, and stay exactly as they were.
          Wired live: `rpc.py`'s `schedule.build_night` and `schedule.
          replan` are real, synchronous, and load-bearing. `schedule_eval.
          py`'s simulator is NOT RPC-exposed -- a single `evaluate_
          scheduling_policies` call takes tens of seconds (many real
          `followup.plan` geometry calls per simulated night), too slow
          for a synchronous RPC round trip without `job.submit`-style
          async wrapping, which was not built this session; it belongs in
          the `job.submit` category the `READY-TO-RUN SCALE STUDIES`
          section below already uses for long-running work, not as a
          direct handler. No UI panel yet, for the same "nothing to browse
          from a live acquisition path" reason the other three 2026-08-29-
          plan entries above already state.
          Genuinely open: the local-search swap in `schedule.py` can, in
          principle, place a candidate outside its own true visibility
          window if two candidates' feasible windows differ (stated in
          `_local_search_reduce_slew`'s own docstring) -- not yet measured
          how often this actually occurs on realistic candidate sets, the
          same class of "declared, not gated" caveat several roadmap items
          above already carry. `RESOLUTION_FRACTION`/
          `RESOLVED_ENTROPY_THRESHOLD` are hand-set constants, not
          calibrated against any real follow-up outcome (none exist in
          this codebase to calibrate against), the same caveat `discard_
          adjudication.py`'s significance thresholds already carry.
          Tests: `tests/test_schedule.py` (13 cases), `tests/
          test_schedule_eval.py` (12 cases, including the measured
          entropy-reduced-vs-resolved-candidates discrepancy and the noise-
          erodes-advantage robustness check), `tests/test_rpc_schedule.py`
          (4 cases). Full suite green afterward: 2473 passed, 56 skipped.

---------------------------------------------------------------------
READY-TO-RUN SCALE STUDIES  <-- 2026-08-19
---------------------------------------------------------------------

Prepared but deliberately NOT started. Each is long-running, and the whole
point of the entries above is that a result is only worth having if the run
behind it was explicit. Submit through `job.submit` so the run survives the
caller and reports progress; the inner params are identical if called direct.

Access is JSON-lines over stdin to `python -m astra`. There is no CLI.

  # Stage-B method comparison at scale, ZTF only, deep models ON.
  # include_deep defaults to FALSE (released installers ship no torch), so a
  # run meant to settle deep-vs-PCA MUST pass it explicitly or it silently
  # compares PCA against nothing.
  {"id":1,"method":"job.submit","params":{"method":"stageb.compare","params":{
     "survey":"ztf","limit":10000,"seeds":[17,29,43,59,71],
     "include_deep":true,"epochs":20,"mode":"time"}}}

  # Same population, season resampling. This is the untested question raised
  # in the resampling entry above: higher representation validity is not the
  # same thing as better injection recovery.
  {"id":2,"method":"job.submit","params":{"method":"stageb.compare","params":{
     "survey":"ztf","limit":10000,"seeds":[17,29,43,59,71],
     "include_deep":true,"epochs":20,"mode":"season"}}}

  # Autoencoder sweep, ZTF only. `survey` matters: a sweep on a pooled
  # ZTF+TESS population tunes for the mixture's separation, not the survey's.
  # Start with a sub-grid via `overrides` -- the full grid is 27 configs x 3
  # seeds = 81 trainings (VAE: 243, transformer: 108).
  {"id":3,"method":"job.submit","params":{"method":"deep.sweep","params":{
     "kind":"autoencoder","survey":"ztf","seeds":[17,29,43],"epochs":20,
     "limit":10000,"overrides":{"latent_dim":[8,32]}}}}

  # Repeated ablation with more seeds. Now checkpointed, so this can be
  # interrupted and resumed. The recorded 5-seed intervals straddle chance
  # for tess_only and gaia_tess; more seeds is the stated fix.
  {"id":4,"method":"job.submit","params":{"method":"ablation.repeated","params":{
     "survey":"ztf","seeds":[17,29,43,59,71,83,97,101,103,107]}}}

  # Artifact weight calibration, recorded. Proposes weights; never adopts.
  {"id":5,"method":"artifact.calibrate","params":{"n_per_class":150,
     "seeds":[17,29,43,59,71],"hard_real_fraction":0.4}}

  # Read results back. Nested metrics need a dotted path.
  {"id":6,"method":"experiment.list","params":{}}
  {"id":7,"method":"experiment.compare","params":{
     "experiment_ids":["EXP-0002","EXP-0003"],"metric":"aggregate.roc_auc.mean"}}
  {"id":8,"method":"experiment.verify","params":{"experiment_id":"EXP-0003"}}

Before starting any of these, fix the parameter set. stageb's checkpoint
invalidates when ANY configuration key changes (limit, mode, epochs,
include_deep, seed list), so a mid-run parameter change silently restarts
from zero rather than resuming. Same for ablation.repeated's checkpoint.

Concurrency: jobs runs two at a time (_EXECUTOR max_workers=2); a third
queues. Feature extraction underneath is capped at 4 workers by measurement,
not by core count -- the limit is memory bandwidth (featurematrix.py:30-43).


---------------------------------------------------------------------
DONE: MULTI-BAND PERIOD SEARCH (fast mode) via sidecar  <-- 2026-08-19
---------------------------------------------------------------------

UPDATE 2026-08-19: built, using the "fast" corner of the design space this
entry originally scoped, after measuring rather than assuming the accurate
corner would be affordable. It was not.

The old `multiband_periodic_features()` implementation described below --
hand-rolled concatenation, per-band weighted-mean subtraction, ONE
Lomb-Scargle over the union, a single shared sinusoid with no per-band
amplitude/phase -- is GONE, replaced in place with a call to
`astropy.timeseries.LombScargleMultiband` (point 1 of the old design,
confirmed already vendored: astropy==8.0.1 has it importable and working).
Same function signature and guards, so it is a drop-in for the one existing
test, which still passes on the new internals.

The measurement that decided the rest of this: at real ZTF production scale
(2 bands, 350 points/band, 2740-day baseline, ~273k frequencies) --
  existing single-band production path:                    1.19 s
  LombScargleMultiband(method="fast"):                      2.35 s
  LombScargleMultiband(method="flexible", the DEFAULT):    38.76 s  (33x)
"flexible" is the genuine joint base+per-band regularised fit -- the one
this entry originally had in mind when it said "per-band amplitude and
phase." It is not used. `featurematrix.py`'s own profiling already
established Lomb-Scargle at ~98% of feature-extraction time and justified
the whole caching/parallelism investment in Phase 9; a 33x multiplier on top
of that is not a cost this pipeline can absorb. `multiband_periodic_features`
now pins `method="fast"` explicitly and RAISES if asked for a "gpu" backend
(rather than silently ignoring it) -- "fast" dispatches to single-band
LombScargle per band internally via its own sb_method, which is not a clean
hook for the CUDA kernel in gpu_periodogram.py without patching astropy
internals. A test (`test_multiband_uses_the_fast_method_never_flexible`)
monkeypatches `LombScargleMultiband.autopower` and asserts the `method` kwarg
it actually receives -- a silent regression to "flexible" here is a 33x
slowdown, not a subtle one, and deserves a loud test, the same way
SAMPLES_PER_PEAK == 5 is pinned against silent regression.
`flexible` remains a deliberately-not-built option: if a future confirmatory
study genuinely needs joint per-band amplitude/phase (not just a combined
SNR/period), it needs either a small bounded population and ~39s/object
patience, or a fundamentally different implementation (e.g. a GPU multiband
kernel) well beyond this pass.

Point 2's group-keying problem (below) is solved exactly as scoped: a new
`multiband.py` module (`build_multiband_sidecar()`) groups stored curves by
(survey, release, object_id) across bands, and writes results through
`modalitymatrix.py` -- the EXISTING keyed-Parquet-sidecar mechanism already
used for image/spectral features, extended by adding "multiband" to
`_safe_kind()`'s allow-list. `KEY_COLUMNS` (survey/release/object_id/band)
was NOT changed -- a multiband row spans a group of bands, not one curve, so
it is written under a sentinel `band="__multiband__"` key instead. Verified
end to end with real synthetic data: a 2-band object correctly gets one
sidecar row with a period recovered to 4.7e-5 relative error; a 1-band
object is correctly skipped entirely (not given a NaN row); `join_rows`
correctly reports which objects actually matched.
This is a genuinely cheaper path than the version-bump alternative: no
change to `FEATURE_NAMES`, no `FEATURE_VERSION` bump, none of point 5's cost
(feature cache, saved matrices, resumable checkpoints, ranker manifests, or
experiment-drift flags) had to move. `stageb.compare`/`ablation.repeated`'s
"explicit RPC job, never run by the default pipeline" pattern is followed
exactly: registered as `features.multiband_build`, never called by
`featurematrix.build()`. Verified live against the real local ZTF store
(50 real objects): every one is genuinely single-band at this scale, so the
run correctly produced zero sidecar rows rather than fabricating anything --
confirmed independently by checking the raw curve files, not assumed to be
a grouping bug.

Point 3 (cross-survey interaction) is honoured by construction, not merely
avoided: `evidence.py`'s per-survey independent period fits
(`SurveyView.best_period_days`, what `score_profile()`'s `period_agreement`
component actually compares) are completely untouched -- `multiband.py`
never calls into `evidence.py` at all. A test asserts
`evidence.WEIGHTS["period_agreement"] == 0.27` is unaffected after a
multiband build runs, and the full `TestEvidenceScoring`/`TestPeriodAgreement`/
`TestPeriodFalseAlarm` suite (19 tests) was re-run and confirmed green.
The multiband sidecar is joinable, additional evidence -- a combined
cross-band SNR/period for objects where no single band alone has enough
points to detect one -- not a replacement for the per-survey comparison.

Point 4 (FAP against a hierarchical/Bayesian posterior) does not apply to
what was actually built: "fast" mode's output is still a periodogram peak on
a uniform frequency grid (per-band fits combined by weight), not a
posterior sample, so `period_agreement_fap()`'s existing null is not being
silently misapplied to it -- the sidecar simply is not fed into that
function at all yet. This remains open only if `flexible` (a genuinely
different, sample-based accuracy profile) is ever built.

Points 5 and 6 (version-bump cost, and the "budget for it" performance
warning) are resolved by the sidecar approach never triggering point 5's
cost at all, and by point 6's warning being exactly why `flexible` was
rejected in favour of `fast`.

UPDATE 2026-08-22: five research-module cards landed (Gaia DR4 multi-band
ingestion, Rubin/LSST TAP, CHIME/FRB, the GW/FRB/neutrino/high-energy event
graph, TESS PSF deblending). Investigation found two of the five already
substantially built (CHIME/FRB in `frb.py`, the event graph in
`association.py`), so this round hardened/exposed those instead of
rebuilding them, and added genuinely new code only where a real gap existed:
  - `surveys/gaia.py`/`surveys/gaia_epoch.py`: `GaiaEpochAdapter` now accepts
    a `bands` parameter (`g`/`bp`/`rp`/`rvs`, still placeholder DR4 column
    names -- no real schema exists yet), `ingest_resumable` threads it
    through with a bumped schema hash so checkpoints from different band
    sets can never mix. Added `epoch_completeness()` and
    `sustained_ingest_throughput()` (the latter using `polars`/`duckdb`,
    previously-pinned-but-unused dependencies, for windowed aggregation).
  - `tap.py`: generalized `query()`'s hardcoded `provider="datalab"` into a
    real `provider`/`auth_header` parameter pair, exactly the two things
    this file's own 2026-08-21 entry named as needed before a Rubin
    connector could exist. New `surveys/rubin_tap.py` (`RubinTAPConnector`,
    `credential_required=True`, `enabled_by_default=False`) is DORMANT --
    written and tested only against mocked TAP responses, since ASTRA still
    has no real Rubin data-rights token; `credentials.rubin.*` RPC handlers
    are wired so the only remaining step once a token exists is calling
    `credentials.save_credentials("rubin", ...)`. ALeRCE's existing
    credential-free LSST route is unchanged and remains the recommended path
    today.
  - `frb.py`/`association.py`: no new statistics. Added
    `tests/test_frb_live.py` and `tests/test_alerts_gcn_live.py` (both
    marked `live`, skipped by default -- see `tests/conftest.py`'s
    `pytest_collection_modifyitems`) to convert the "re-verify once CADC's
    outage clears" and "GCN per-instrument filter not yet confirmed" caveats
    into runnable checks instead of TODO comments. Added
    `events.graph.correlate`/`events.graph.calibrate` RPC handlers exposing
    `association.event_to_event_correlation`/`calibrate_event_graph` (this
    was the one real exposure gap -- `events.associate`, the event-to-
    candidate path, was already wired). Added a golden-fixture regression
    test using the real published GW170817/GRB170817A coincidence
    (`tests/test_event_graph.py::TestGoldenFixtureGW170817`).
  - New `tess_psf.py`: a genuine forward PSF scene model, the one module in
    this round with no existing implementation to extend.
    `photutils.psf.CircularGaussianPRF`/`PSFPhotometry` (new dependency,
    `photutils==3.0.0`) jointly fits per-cadence flux for the target plus
    every in-cutout Gaia neighbor with positions held FIXED (deblending at
    known positions, not source detection). Ships with
    `flux_rmse`/`blend_attribution_accuracy`/`injected_source_recovery`
    (Monte-Carlo, separation/flux-ratio grid) as the falsifiable validation
    the card asked for. Not wired into `extract_photometry`'s aperture
    output or into `scoring.WEIGHTS` -- new evidence only, same discipline
    as everything else in this list. One real pitfall found and fixed while
    building this: a hand-written Gaussian formula for synthetic test
    images looked reasonable but silently disagreed with
    `CircularGaussianPRF`'s own pixel-integration behaviour at TESS's
    narrow (~1.5px FWHM) scale, producing a ~20% spurious recovery bias that
    had nothing to do with the deblending machinery being tested --
    `tess_psf._synthetic_gaussian_image` now builds injected scenes from the
    exact model class the fitter uses, not a parallel reimplementation.


---------------------------------------------------------------------
ROADMAP ITEMS 26-30 (panchromatic SED, 3-D dust, polarization, strong
lensing, weak lensing)  <-- NEW 2026-08-25/26
---------------------------------------------------------------------

[PARTIAL] Panchromatic energy-balance SED (item 26, P1)
          Four new metadata-only survey connectors -- `surveys/galex.py`,
          `surveys/twomass.py`, `surveys/wise.py`, `surveys/herschel.py`
          -- extend `sed.py`'s existing Gaia/ZTF/TESS optical-only reach
          into UV and IR. Each targets a real, VizieR-hosted catalogue
          confirmed live this session via the same `vizier.cds.unistra.fr`
          Simple Cone Search endpoint `vlass.py` already uses:
          `II/335/galex_ais` (GALEX GUVcat AIS GR6+7), `II/246/out` (2MASS
          PSC), `II/328/allwise` (AllWISE), `VIII/106` (Herschel/PACS
          point sources). All four have live-marked fixture tests
          (`tests/test_surveys_{galex,twomass,wise,herschel}.py`) that
          actually ran against the real service this session; Herschel's
          live test needed a real PACS-covered field (ECDFS,
          RA=53.13/Dec=-27.80) after RA=180/Dec=0 -- every other
          connector's live-test coordinate -- returned zero rows,
          confirmed live, because PACS is pointed, not all-sky.
          `sed_energy_balance.py` adds the actual energy-balance
          diagnostic: `energy_balance_residual()` is a DISTANCE-
          INDEPENDENT ratio of dust-reprocessed (mid/far-IR) to absorbed
          (UV-optical) integrated nu*F_nu, using standard published
          photometric zero points (GALEX AB; 2MASS Cohen+2003; WISE
          Wright+2010/Jarrett+2011) -- deliberately not an absolute-
          luminosity SED fit, since a general candidate has no reliable
          distance. `temperature_extinction_bias()` compares `sed.
          characterize`'s fitted temperature with vs. without UV bands on
          synthetic injected ground truth (the "temperature/extinction
          bias" metric). `evaluate_class_likelihood_calibration()` is a
          bounded `LogisticRegression` probe (same shape as
          `sn_classification_eval._macro_f1`) over synthetic
          class-conditional (energy-ratio, color) distributions for
          star/dusty-star-forming/AGN-continuum classes -- validates the
          classifier MECHANISM only, not a trained real-population model.
          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`/`scoring.WEIGHTS`/`evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_sed_energy_balance.py` (11 cases) plus the
          four connector test files above (32 offline + 4 live cases).

[PARTIAL] 3-D dust marginalization (item 27, P1)
          Gaia parallaxes reused unchanged via `surveys/gaia.py`. Neither
          dust-map source this item originally named turned out reachable
          -- checked live, not assumed: `stilism.obspm.fr` fails DNS
          resolution outright, and the legacy Bayestar web API at
          `argonaut.skymaps.info` is a stale Apache/2.2 CentOS host
          returning 404 on every documented endpoint. The real substitute,
          confirmed live: VizieR's `J/A+A/664/A174` (Vergely, Lallement &
          Cox 2022, A&A 664, A174 -- the direct successor to STILISM from
          the same research group), served as plain FITS density cubes
          from `cdsarc.cds.unistra.fr` (confirmed via a live HEAD request
          and a partial-header download). `dust_3d.py`'s
          `fetch_dust_cube()`/`load_dust_cube()` download and cache the
          050pc-resolution cube (~41 MB, new `cdsarc` netclient bucket);
          `extinction_profile()` trilinearly integrates density along a
          line of sight; `distance_posterior()` combines a Bailer-Jones
          et al. (2018) exponential space-density prior with a Gaussian
          parallax likelihood; `marginalize_extinction()` is the actual
          distance-extinction posterior this item names.
          A real bug was found and fixed this session:
          `astropy.io.fits` hands back cube data as
          `(NAXIS3, NAXIS2, NAXIS1)`, not `(NAXIS1, NAXIS2, NAXIS3)` --
          `load_dust_cube` now transposes it back. The map's Galactic-
          Cartesian axis convention (X=l=0/b=0, Y=l=90, Z=NGP) was
          checked, not just assumed, with a real full-cube download this
          session: cumulative extinction to 500pc toward the Galactic
          plane (0.93 mag) came out ~18x higher than toward the Galactic
          pole (0.05 mag), the expected qualitative contrast for a real
          dust distribution concentrated in the thin disk -- confirming
          the axes are not swapped or mirrored. This is a qualitative
          sanity check, not a quantitative validation against one named
          star's spectroscopically measured extinction, which remains
          open. Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT
          wired into `rpc.py`/`scoring.WEIGHTS`/`evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_dust_3d.py` (13 offline cases against a
          synthetic uniform-density cube -- LOS-integrator linearity,
          Bailer-Jones posterior peak location, marginalized-extinction
          recovery -- plus 1 live case that downloads and loads the real
          cube).

[PARTIAL] Polarization feature pipeline (item 28, P2)
          Neither a generic "optical polarimetry archive" nor "radio
          Faraday data" service had one obvious credential-free API;
          `Vizier.find_catalogs` (the same discovery tool `vlass.py`
          already used) surfaced two real, live-confirmed substitutes:
          `J/ApJ/728/104` (Santos et al. 2011, optical polarization for
          878 Hipparcos stars; real `PV`/`PA` columns) and
          `J/other/RAA/14.942` (Xu & Han 2014, radio rotation measures;
          real `RM` column). `polarization_features.py` exposes both as
          bounded query helpers (`vlass.query_nvss_flux_1_4ghz`'s shape,
          not a full `SurveyConnector` -- neither is a light-curve/
          catalogue-membership survey), plus `stokes_from_p_theta`/
          `p_theta_from_stokes` (verified round-trip in tests) and
          `polarization_angle_error_deg` (the standard Serkowski 1958/
          Wardle & Kronberg 1974 `28.65*sigma_p/p` formula -- the
          "polarization-angle error" metric this item names).
          `evaluate_class_separability()` is the same bounded logistic-
          probe pattern as item 26's class-likelihood check, over
          synthetic (polarization degree, |RM|, photometric-variability
          amplitude) features -- TESS/ZTF timing folded in only as that
          third feature dimension, not as new acquisition. Like every
          other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired into
          `rpc.py`/`scoring.WEIGHTS`/`evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_polarization_features.py` (16 offline cases
          plus 2 live cases confirming both query helpers reach real
          services).

[PARTIAL] Strong-lens morphology and time-delay inference (item 29, P2)
          DES/Pan-STARRS/Hubble/JWST connectors already exist. HSC has no
          VizieR-hosted point-source catalogue that resolved live this
          session; the real catalogue cross-check used instead is
          `J/A+A/688/A34` ("Strong lenses KiDS DR4", Grespan et al. 2024),
          confirmed live. `strong_lens.py` implements the singular
          isothermal ellipsoid (SIE) lens equation in closed form
          (Kormann, Schneider & Bartelmann 1994) -- the "differentiable
          lens equation" this item names, differentiable in the sense
          that every piece is a plain closed-form expression
          `scipy.optimize` already takes gradients through. The SIE
          lensing POTENTIAL used for the time-delay calculation is
          derived (not separately cited) from Euler's homogeneous-
          function theorem (`psi(theta) = theta . alpha(theta)`, exact
          for a 0-homogeneous deflection field) and verified against a
          numerical gradient of `alpha` in `tests/test_strong_lens.py`,
          not merely asserted. `solve_image_positions()` root-finds
          multiply-imaged solutions; `fit_lens_model()` recovers
          `(theta_e, axis_ratio, position_angle, beta)` from observed
          image positions via least squares. A real bug was found and
          fixed this session: `scipy.optimize.least_squares(method="lm")`
          refuses to run when a two-image ("double") system's 4 residuals
          are fewer than the fit's 5 unknowns -- switched to `method=
          "trf"`, which handles the underdetermined case; the recovery
          study (`strong_lens_eval.py`) additionally restricts itself to
          3+-image ("quad"-like) systems, the same restriction real
          strong-lens modelling applies to doubles without an external
          constraint. `time_delay_seconds()` is the standard Fermat-
          potential time-delay formula (Refsdal 1964; Treu & Marshall
          2016 review). `strong_lens_eval.py` provides the three named
          metrics on synthetic injected lenses: `evaluate_lens_auprc()`
          (lensed = source inside the caustic vs. unlensed = source far
          outside, scored by solved image count), `evaluate_
          image_position_residual()`, and `evaluate_time_delay_error()`.
          Like every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired
          into `rpc.py`/`scoring.WEIGHTS`/`evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_strong_lens.py` (16 offline + 1 live case)
          and `tests/test_strong_lens_eval.py` (6 cases).

[PARTIAL] Weak-lensing environment features (item 30, P2)
          DES exists; HSC-SSP has no per-galaxy shear catalogue reachable
          the way DES/Pan-STARRS are (only a shear-selected CLUSTER
          sample, `J/PASJ/73/817`, surfaced via `Vizier.find_catalogs`).
          KiDS is the real substitute confirmed live: `II/384`
          ("KiDS-450: Weak lensing shear measurements", Hildebrandt et
          al. 2017) -- a real cone search returned real `e1`/`e2`/
          `Weight`/`MultCal` rows. `weak_lensing.py`'s
          `query_kids_shear_catalog()` exposes it; `calibrate_shear_bias()`
          recovers multiplicative/additive `(m, c)` bias via weighted
          least squares on the standard `e_obs=(1+m)*g_true+c` model,
          validated against synthetic injected `(m, c)`.
          `tangential_shear_profile()` is the standard stacked
          tangential-/cross-shear halo-environment observable (Mandelbaum
          2018 review); validated against a synthetic pure-E-mode
          injection recovering the injected tangential amplitude with
          near-zero cross-shear (the standard B-mode null test).
          `environment_density_shear_correlation()` is a diagnostic
          Pearson correlation between local source density and shear
          amplitude -- correlation only, no halo mass inferred. Like
          every other opt-in research module in this file, NOT wired into
          `rpc.py`/`scoring.WEIGHTS`/`evidence.py`.
          Tests: `tests/test_weak_lensing.py` (13 offline + 1 live case).

This batch's full offline suite (`pytest tests` from the repo root) was
re-run after all five items landed: 1892 passed, 33 skipped (the
pre-existing `live`/optional-dependency skips), 0 failed.
